The SLB Name, Marks, and Copyright

## 1.0 SLB—both a company name and a trademark

The word “SLB” is both a trade name (used to identify the company and its business) and a trademark (used to identify the source of goods and services). Both have important roles in promoting the SLB brand, which is the continuously evolving corporate image discerned by our customers, our shareholders, and the industry.

The rules for correct use of the trade name and trademark are different. These rules must be carefully adhered to because improper use can reduce the value of a trademark.

## 1.1 Correct use of the SLB trade name and SLB trademark

The word “SLB” can be used correctly as a noun (trade name) or as an adjective (trademark) but cannot be used as a verb.

SLB trade name usage

## 1.1.1 As a trade name, use the word “SLB” only as a noun.

Correct: SLB creates technology that unlocks access to energy for the benefit of all.

## 1.1.2 As a trade name, “SLB” is used as a singular noun.

Incorrect: SLB have announced management changes. Correct: SLB has announced management changes.

Incorrect: Worldwide SLBs provide services for the life of the reservoir.

Correct: SLB offers worldwide access to new technology. 1.1.3 The possessive form is correct only when “SLB” is used as a trade name. Do not form a possessive if “SLB” is used as a trademark to designate the source of a product, service, technology, or solution.

Incorrect: SLB’s new tool was launched in Indonesia last week.

Correct: The new SLB tool was launched in Indonesia last week.

Correct: SLB’s stock increased in value this year. Correct: SLB’s business philosophy encompasses teamwork and cultural diversity.

SLB trademark usage

## 1.1.4 As a trademark, use the word “SLB” only as an adjective modifying a noun.

Incorrect: SLB’s log was acquired before the hole collapsed. Correct: The SLB log was acquired before the hole collapsed.

Correct: SLB technology is supported by a global team of experts.

## 1.1.5 Never modify the word “SLB” or use it in coined words.

Incorrect: e-SLB, SLBlog Correct: SLB e-commerce, SLB log Combinations, formatting, and other common uses of the word “SLB” 1.1.6 “SLB” can be used as a trade name in combination with other trade names and trademarks. Standard grammar rules apply.

Incorrect: The Shell/SLB alliance was formed in 1999. Correct: The Shell-SLB alliance was formed in 1999.

1.1.7 In copy, “SLB” is always written in capital letters. Lowercased “slb” is used only in the company logo and as a component in electronic addresses, including the public website slb.com. Do not use “Schlumberger” as the spelled-out version of “SLB,” as “SLB” is not an initialism.

Incorrect: Schlumberger today announced a merger with a leading manufacturer.

Correct: SLB (NYSE: SLB) today announced the acquisition of a leading manufacturer.

Incorrect: Schlumberger personnel are located around the world.

Correct: SLB personnel are located around the world. Correct: For more information on stimulation, go to slb.com or contact us at stiminfo@slb.com.

## 1.1.8 SLB is always pronounced as individual letters.

1.1.9 Routine mentions of “Schlumberger” that require updating should be replaced with “SLB.” If necessary, when “Schlumberger” is discussed in the context of its history, the phrase “Schlumberger (now SLB)” may be used. For names of physical buildings, foundations, or other instances that use “Schlumberger” in recognition of the company’s founders or otherwise long-standing history, some exceptions may apply and should be verified with the brand department.

Incorrect: Schlumberger has extensive expertise across the oilfield life cycle.

Correct: Schlumberger (now SLB) was founded in 1926. Incorrect: The global oilfield enterprise now known as Schlumberger owes its vitality to more than 100,000 people in 100+ countries.

Correct: SLB has extensive expertise across the oilfield life cycle.

## 1.2 Companies acquired by SLB

1.2.1 Companies acquired by SLB, including associated products and services, integrate into the SLB masterbrand. The section summarizes how companies acquired by SLB are to be classified and defined. For detailed information and processes, refer to the SLB Masterbrand Architecture (also available on the Brand myHub page). This framework organizes and unifies our brand, and all the products and services in our portfolio, inclusive of our acquired brands, so that they are easy to understand, navigate, and buy.

The equity in the names of companies acquired by SLB is considered during the integration process. Division Marcoms should be consulted to determine if key elements are to be retained to respect the acquired company’s strong heritage while aligning their product portfolios under the SLB master brand. It important to recognize that each brand requires its own, well-thought-out approach. Individual logos (the visual identities of those legacy brands) have been fully retired to ensure that SLB goes to market as one brand identity without losing the value of those product names. Additionally, to align with this strategy, do not refer to acquired companies as “an SLB company.” Anything deemed as an exception is referred to the Brand Council.

Acquired Company Classification Definition Example For examples of companies that fit each classification, refer to the SLB Masterbrand Architecture.

Descriptive (nonmarked) names for new product families unless identified as an “exception” as defined in the “SLB Masterbrand Architecture” New product and service families must adopt descriptive names. Assume the SLB masterbrand visual identity.

Family name: Sand consolidation fluids Product or service within the family: Water-based sand consolidation system Usage note: Both family name and products and services within the family use descriptive (nonmarked) names.

Proprietary product family names identified as an “exception” as defined in the “SLB Masterbrand Architecture” The current name has high value or equity as a product company name. Retain names only. Assume the SLB Masterbrand visual identity.

Family name: Cameron Wellhead Systems Product or service: Cameron Fontus configurable compact wellhead system Usage note: Cameron is the proprietary brand treated only as an adjective preceding the product or service; “Fontus” is the marked product name; and “configurable compact wellhead system” is the identifier.

## 2.0 SLB marks and companies’ marks

SLB marks are trademarked names of the company’s products and services. Marks are approved by Brand Checkpoint and maintained by the central Marketing Communications (MarCom) department in coordination with the Division MarComs. Collectively referred to as the “marks list,” published on myHub (http://markslist.slb.com/), it is the definitive source for marks used to correctly identify products and services in public-facing materials. There is also a nonmarks list, which contains a list of descriptive product names. These lists are for internal publication and distribution only.

It is always necessary to reference the marks list for guidance on consistent and correct use in brand or marketing material. The marks list also includes additional information about the history of particular marks. The marks list contains active marks and retired (legacy) marks; the latter are those used to identify SLB goods or services that are no longer commercially. Only the Division that owns a retired mark can restore it to active status; other Divisions cannot repurpose retired marks that are not theirs. Whether a mark is active or retired, it must be used correctly.

Proper Use of Marks Each mark has an identifier, which is a descriptive phrase ending in a noun. The identifier listed on the marks list is considered the “long” or “full” identifier. A mark must always be accompanied by an identifier. There may also be a usage guide for how to use a particular mark, a list of any products within a family mark, or how to shorten the identifier after its initial introduction in the copy. For mark attribution(s) (TM symbol and copyright usage), refer to the guidelines in section 2.1.2 Correct usage.

For questions or updates regarding the marks and nonmarks lists, contact the MarCom editorial team.

## 2.1 Marks of SLB and SLB companies

SLB has both registered and unregistered trademarks, which identify the source of goods, and registered and unregistered service marks, which identify the source of services. All are referred to as “marks.”

## 2.1.1 Naming of SLB marks and usage guidelines

Understanding how SLB product names are created, organized, and used is vital in maintaining and promoting the company’s brand. SLB Marketing Communication helps to maintain and position our products and product names. Implementing this includes how product names are developed, as well as how these names are treated in marketing collateral. This section provides the name usage guidelines most types of marketing material. a variety of material. Refer to the detailed guidance in the SLB Naming Process and Convention Guide.

SLB product names are organized into the three naming categories: descriptive, retained, and proprietary names. Many of these names identify a family of products, and these product families can fall into any category. A product family is described as a collection of related products, services, applications, tools, software, or solutions that share a single name.

Name categories Category Definition Example Descriptive names • Apply to new products without current brand equity • Include new product families • Do not require a trademark symbol and are always lowercase as indicated by the nonmarks list Example: 3D far-field sonic service • Assumes the Masterbrand verbal identity by using a descriptive name that is representative of the actual offering • Easy to understand what the product is/does and where it fits in our offering • Does not use the trademark symbol and follows the lowercased capitalization as shown on the nonmarks list Retained names • Have existing equity in the product name • Retain the product (family) name identified on marks list Example: Litho Scanner™ high-definition spectroscopy service • Assumes Masterbrand verbal identity by using a marked name that is

All proposals to create a new product family name must be referred to the Brand Council. This process can be initiated through the Comms and Branding Support portal. Any exceptions to naming are reviewed and approved by Brand Checkpoint.

## 2.1.2 Correct use

Correct and consistent use of a mark strengthens its marketing value and enables SLB to protect its right to the mark. Use the following guidelines and also refer to special uses in Section 3, “Marks in tables, titles and headings, figures, digital material, and slides."

2.1.2.1 Marks should be used exactly as they appear on http://markslist.slb.com/, paying close attention to capitalization, punctuation, font, and special treatment of components. Marks can be composed of one or more letters, characters, numerals, or a combination of these; marks can also be words, designs (e.g., artwork, graphics, logos, and icons, although Divisions, services, and marks themselves cannot have a logo or icon), or slogans. • Require a trademark symbol and capitalization according to the marks list representative of the actual offering and an identifier that is descriptive of the service • Has existing equity in the product name from its commercialization in May 2012 • Uses a trademark symbol immediately following the mark name on first use and follows the capitalization as shown on the marks list Proprietary names Have existing equity from an acquired company name, OR • new product families that are large enough and differentiated enough to warrant a proprietary name, along with the marketing commitment to invest ($$) in building recognition and equity • require a naming proposal to Brand Council to discuss how the names and products from the company will be added to the marks list, nonmarks list, or dropped entirely Example: GeothermEx™ geothermal consulting services • Assumes the Masterbrand verbal identity by becoming an SLB mark after company acquisition. These proprietary names must never be used as entities or company names themselves. By becoming a mark, they are treated as adjectives that precede a noun or noun phrase • Has existing equity in geothermal domain • Uses a trademark symbol immediately following the mark name on first use and follows the capitalization as shown on the marks list

## 2.1.2.2 Mark attribution(s) (TM symbol and copyright usage)

Video These rules apply for using the ™ for marks within videos (live action or animated):

On first mention of an SLB or SLB-company mark (verbally or visually), an attribution is required in the form of a superscript nonregistered trademark symbol (™).

The ™ symbol should always be placed immediately following the mark on the mark’s first use.

If a mark is introduced in an opening title card, the ™ symbol will suffice as its first use. If there is no opening title card, use a ™ symbol the first time the mark is shown onscreen. The ™ symbol should always be used on the ending title card if there is a mark used for the title or subtitle (even if a ™ symbol was used on the first use earlier in the video, use the ™ symbol on the ending title card if a mark is present).

The “XXX is a mark of SLB” copyright line is no longer needed if a ™ symbol is used or if there are no marks present in a video. The exception to this rule is when a mark is only mentioned verbally and never shown onscreen –or– when a mark is only present as the short URL name. For example, if a mark is part of the voiceover but is never written out onscreen, then include the copyright line, “XXX is a mark of SLB”. When only one mark is present, it will be written as “[Mark] is a mark of SLB.” If two marks are present, “[Mark] and [Mark] are marks of SLB. If three or more marks are present, “[Mark], [Mark], and [Mark] are marks of SLB.

Template examples and detailed instructions for video title and ending cards, including mark attributions, are available in the Media Bank Design Template in the SLB Starter Motion Package (slide 16). If necessary, refer to the video split screen template.

If you have any questions or a situation that’s not covered by these rules, contact Patric Perez at pperez19@slb.com.

Electronic, printed or print-ready, or PowerPoint collateral On first mention of an SLB or SLB-company mark within text, a superscript nonregistered trademark symbol (™) is placed immediately following the mark. Even if the mark is legally registered through the US Patent and Trademark Office (USPTO), use the nonregistered trademark symbol. The symbol is not placed after the mark when the mark appears for the first time in a title or heading; rather, it is placed at the first use of the mark in subsequent text, e.g., body copy. An exception is if the mark appears in only a headline, e.g., a poster or PPT slide, in which case the symbol is placed after its first and only use in the headline.

In printed or print-ready material (e.g., PDF, product sheets), the nonregistered trademark symbol is accompanied by the following copyright or attribution note, or both.

## 2.1.2.3 A mark is used only as an adjective, except where noted for special use cases.

Marks and identifiers with superscript trademark symbols or subscripts Correct: eXpandBG™ near-wellbore to reservoir-scale modeling software application for seismic interpretation (BG is a superscript trademark symbol.)

Correct: InSitu CO2™ reservoir fluid CO2 sensor (The 2 is not subscript in the mark but is in the identifier.)

On first mention of a mark in text The mark should be followed by the identifier noun or noun phrase as listed with it in the marks list.

The identifier should be in lowercase, except for any proper nouns within it. Subsequent mentions of the mark can use the last word of the full identifier as a shortened version of it (also called a short identifier.) Use of nouns or noun phrases that are not in the identifier is not permitted.

Incorrect: ABC™ delivers optimal solutions for operators. This brochure describes the growing market for ABC™ technology.

Correct: ABC™ analysis behind casing services deliver optimal solutions for operators. This brochure describes the growing market for ABC services. (The noun phrase “analysis behind casing services” is the spelled-out identifier from the marks list and the last word “services” is the short identifier.)

Correct: The PowerDrive™ rotary steerable system is available in different sizes. The steerable PowerDrive RSS increases the efficiency of rotary drilling applications. The various BHAs of the PowerDrive system are often used in horizontal wells. (“Rotary steerable system” is the identifier from the marks list, “RSS” is a stand-alone abbreviation for the identifier phrase, and “system” is the short identifier.)

Correct: NATCO DUAL FREQUENCY™ electrostatic treaters, coalescers, and desalters use the load-responsive NATCO LRC-II™ smart interface to deliver industry-leading NATCO™ separation technology, which typically provides process improvement that more than doubles the performance of conventional approaches. (NATCO DUAL FREQUENCY, NATCO LRC-II, and NATCO are individual marks, each with its own identifier.)

When a mark includes a string of nouns In these cases, you may choose one as a short identifier, depending on the sentence’s message and context.

Correct, first mention: NATCO DUAL FREQUENCY™ electrostatic treaters, coalescers, and desalters Correct, subsequent mention: NATCO DUAL FREQUENCY electrostatic treater Correct, subsequent mention: NATCO DUAL FREQUENCY electrostatic coalescer Correct, subsequent mention: NATCO DUAL FREQUENCY electrostatic desalter When a family mark has specific applications, such as services, tools, or solutions, etc.

A family mark can substitute its identifier with a specific application of the mark, such as a distinctive service, solution, tool, or component within that family. An application must always be preceded by the family mark and cannot be used on is own. In other words, the family mark can be paired with either its identifier or one of its specific applications as the identifier.

ReSOLVE iX™ intelligent extreme wireline intervention service Applications include: active debris removal tool, anchor-linear actuator, machining tool, milling tool, setting tool, slot cutter, smart shifting tool, tubing cutter.

ReSOLVE iX™ tubing cutter When a mark includes the name of another mark The included mark is not considered the first mention and is not attributed for that mention.

The NATCO™ Jewel T™ hydrocarbon dew point control system recovers valuable hydrocarbon liquids while preparing gas to meet pipeline specifications. Jewel T systems are designed for high-pressure remote well services where centralized gas gathering facilities are not available or practical.

(The first mention of the Jewel T mark must be prefaced with the NATCO mark.

The NATCO mark is attributed for this as its first mention.) When a mark is a software Marks for software are allowed to substitute their descriptively named modules (which include plug- ins and apps but not workflows) or the date-based version number for the identifier. Due to this unique relationship, a module, plug-in, or app name can never be used on its own; it must always be preceded by the mark.

For software only Correct, allowed on first use: Petrel™ Reservoir Engineering seamlessly links to INTERSECT™ 2018.1 and established numeric simulators.

(The marks list identifier is Petrel subsurface software, and the application name is Petrel Reservoir Engineering.

The marks list identifier is INTERSECT high-resolution reservoir simulator, and

It is recommended to avoid using the nonregistered superscript trademark symbol within a mark and module pairing. To satisfy a first mention, state the mark™ and the full identifier of the parent software before naming the module. the date-based version is INTERSECT 2018.1.)

Correct, preferred on first use: The Petrel™ subsurface software includes Petrel Reservoir Engineering.

(Petrel subsurface software is the parent software that includes Petrel Reservoir Engineering, the application name.)

Special use for all software on the Delfi digital platform This includes both marked software and unmarked application or module names.

When the Delfi™ mark is used with other software marks or unmarked application names or domain profiles, the phrase “on Delfi” is appended at the end. When the Delfi mark is combined with other software marks, use of the identifier for the other marked software is preferred following the “on Delfi” phrase.

Subsequent use of the software mark or unmarked application name omits the “on Delfi” phrase.

Correct for marked software on the Delfi platform only (examples):

Option 1, first use (preferred): DrillOps™ on Delfi™, an on-target well delivery solution Option 1, subsequent use (preferred):

DrillOps solution (Superscript trademark symbol nonregistered trademark symbols are used for both marks on first mention.

Subsequent use drops the “on Delfi” phrase and uses at least the marked software’s short identifier.)

Option 2, first use: DrillOps™ on Delfi™ Option 2, subsequent use: DrillOps on- target well delivery solution (Subsequent use drops the “on Delfi” phrase and must use the full identifier of the marked software, because it was not previously introduced.)

Correct for marked software on the Delfi platform only, first mention for press releases and material for print (examples):

Option 1, first use (preferred: ProcessOps™ on Delfi™, an assured process optimization solution, enhances facility performance and operations uptime.

Option 1, subsequent use (preferred): With ProcessOps solution, your facility’s approach to maintenance no longer needs to be preventive or reactive and always playing catch-up.

(Nonregistered superscript trademark symbols are used for both marks on first mention. Subsequent use drops the “on Delfi” phrase and uses at least the marked software’s short identifier.)

Option 2, first use: ProcessOps™ on Delfi™ provides key insights for asset management and process optimization across the facility space.

Option 2, subsequent use: ProcessOps assured process optimization solution also powers exploring your future performance options and setting priorities.

(Superscript trademark symbol nonregistered trademark symbols are used for both marks on first mention.

Subsequent use drops the “on Delfi” phrase and must use the full identifier of the marked software, because it was not previously introduced.)

Correct for unmarked applications or domain profiles on the Delfi platform only (examples):

First use: Drilling Interpretation on Delfi™ Subsequent use: Drilling Interpretation (Subsequent use drops the “on Delfi” phrase.)

First use: Petrophysicist on Delfi™ Subsequent use: Petrophysicist

(Subsequent use drops the “on Delfi” phrase.) First use: Reservoir Engineering on Delfi Subsequent use: Reservoir Engineering (Subsequent use drops the “on Delfi” phrase.)

When a mark has new generations A generational phrase can be used with a mark instead of creating a new mark. The generational phrase must precede the mark and cannot be added to the identifier.

Incorrect: TRC-II™ second-generation tubing retrievable charged safety valve Correct: second-generation TRC-II™ tubing-retrievable charged safety valve When a mark uses models or version numbers Model or version numbers are allowed between the mark and the identifier to denote incremental changes for products or services, instead of creating a new brand identity or mark.

If the model is designated by an alphanumeric, it cannot be used within the mark and identifier pair.

Incorrect: PowerJet™ model 2506 deep- penetrating shaped charge Correct: PowerJet™ 2506 deep- penetrating shaped charge Correct: The PowerJet Nova™ 4505 extradeep penetrating shaped charge significantly outperforms previous- generation shaped charges.

Incorrect: LEDEEN™ Model HS actuators Incorrect: LEDEEN™ HS actuators Incorrect: LEDEEN HS™ actuators Correct: model HS LEDEEN™ actuators Correct: LEDEEN™ actuator model HS Adjectives or other modifiers cannot interrupt the mark and identifier pair.

Incorrect: LEDEEN™ scotch-yoke actuators Correct: Scotch-yoke LEDEEN™ actuators Correct: LEDEEN™ actuators featuring a scotch-yoke design Marks as initialisms or acronyms Correct: ACQG™ advanced crystal quartz gauge Correct: BEST™ buildup evaluation shut-in tool

## 2.1.2.4 Never use a mark in possessive or plural form.

Incorrect: eFire’s advanced technology offers a significant safety advantage.

Correct: The advanced technology employed by the eFire™ electronic firing head system provides a significant safety advantage.

Incorrect: Three CT EXPRESSes™ were used in the field. Correct: Three CT EXPRESS™ rapid-deployment coiled tubing units were used in the field.

2.1.2.5 Never use an engineering code name, field term, or internal mnemonic for a product or service that has a mark.

Incorrect: The sensors of the PSP production services platform are used to identify multiphase flow.

Correct: The sensors of the PS Platform™ production logging services platform are used to identify multiphase flow.

Incorrect: PEX measurements set a new benchmark for accuracy and efficiency.

Correct: The combination of wireline measurements from the Platform Express™ integrated wireline logging tool sets a new benchmark for accuracy and efficiency.

## 2.1.2.6 Never form a compound by using an SLB mark and another company’s trade name,

trademark, or product name. See Section 2.2 for guidelines on the use of other companies’ marks.

Incorrect: The OrientXact™-Aqwanot® completion successfully controlled sand and water production.

Correct: Completion with the OrientXact™ tubing-conveyed oriented perforating system and an Aqwanot® downhole oil and water separator successfully controlled sand and water production.

2.1.2.7 Marks may also be company slogans. Marks in this category are the only marks that can routinely stand alone and that are not used exclusively as adjectives.

Correct: Apply everywhere what we learn anywhere™ illustrates the company philosophy of sharing best practices and lessons learned.

2.1.2.8 For materials submitted to other scientific or technical publications or conferences, overt commercialism is prohibited. The publication or conference organization requirements and guidelines must be followed. Items deemed to be commercial in nature will not be allowed or disseminated per SLB’s MyPaper3 protocol and review process. The following guidelines apply when preparing any material for presentation or publication, including content for SPE submissions: • Avoid any form of commercial promotion or advertising. Replace commercial trade names or trademarked material with generic descriptions whenever possible. Instead of using a SLB mark name, use its identifier. • Refrain from using brand names, company logos, trademarks, or any branding, including in figures and tables. • Ensure the text is purely informational and educational in tone or intent. Focus on delivering value through research findings, technical insights, and industry knowledge.

If the organization does not specify guidelines, the guidelines for SPE apply. Adhering to these guidelines maintains the integrity and professionalism of SLB’s technical content.

## 2.2 Other companies’ marks

Marketing materials may contain marks of other companies, marks held jointly by SLB and other companies through joint ventures, and marks for products distributed by or under license to SLB.

When marks of other companies are mentioned, the following copyright line can be used at the bottom of the collateral: "Other company, product, and service names are the properties of their respective owners." Because we may not always be aware of a particular company’s legal status, or whether companies have changed ownership or are in the process of being acquired (either now or in the future), the copyright statement ensures our collateral remains current. Note: the copyright line is not required on SLB.com web pages, which contain a Terms of Service.

Special uses

## 2.2.1 Other companies’ marks can be identified with conventional trademark or footnote

symbols and referenced in the attribution note. If symbols are used, a registered trademark is identified on first use with the registered trademark symbol (®), and a trademark or service that is not registered is identified with a superscript trademark symbol TM (™) or SM (SM), respectively. The symbols for ®, ™, and SM are not treated as note reference symbols, such as those used in footnotes. Their function is to identify trademark status.

If symbols are not used to identify marks of other companies, the marks can be named in an attribution note.

## 2.2.2 Marks that are held jointly by SLB and other companies as well as other companies’

marks may require different treatment from what is stated in Section 2.1.2.2 because of SLB contracts. Refer to “Writing and Style” for the footnote symbol hierarchy. Note: In joint ventures, SLB has only a portion of ownership, warranting the different usage guidance.

Correct: Alternate Path® is a registered trademark of ExxonMobil Corp.; technology licensed to SLB. (Wording is per SLB-ExxonMobil contract.)

2.2.3 In collateral that does not have a title page, attribution notes for other companies’ marks are placed at the end of the document.

Correct: Alternate Path® is a registered trademark of ExxonMobil Corp.; technology licensed to SLB. (Wording is per SLB-ExxonMobil contract.)

Other company, product, and service names are the properties of their respective owners.

2.2.4 In a document that has a title page, such as a book, attributions for marks that have contractually specified wording can be placed on the back of the title page if the list does not cause a layout problem. They can be either grouped in paragraph style or placed on separate lines. List the marks in order alphabetically by the first mark for each company.

Terminal punctuation is used at the end of each line if notes are on separate lines and is used between groups of marks if marks are placed in paragraph style.

Incorrect: AllPAC® and AllFRAC® are registered trademarks of ExxonMobil Corp. for which SLB holds a license.

Correct: AllFRAC® and AllPAC® are marks of ExxonMobil Corp.; technology licensed to

## SLB.

2.2.5 In a document that has only a few marks that have contractually specified attribution wording, the accompanying notes can be placed at the bottom of the page where the marks appear.

## 3.0 Marks in tables, titles and headings, figures,

digital materials, and slides Complete information on the correct use of marks and their identifying trademark symbols (or other symbols) and attribution notes is covered in Section 2.0 SLB marks and companies’ marks.

The following instructions give exceptions to or clarification of the guidelines. Also, refer to SLB Brand Guidelines located in Media Bank for related graphic standards for marketing materials.

## 3.1 Tables, table titles, and figure captions

Table copy, table titles, and captions to figures are considered text, and a superscript unregistered trademark symbol is placed after the SLB mark if its first mention occurs in a table or caption. The short version of the identifier following the first mention of a mark in tables may be used to accommodate space limitations.

## 3.2 Document titles and headings and ad headlines

The superscript nonregistered trademark symbol is not placed after the mark when the mark appears for the first time in a title or heading; rather, it is placed at the first use of the mark in subsequent text, e.g., body copy. An exception is if the mark appears in only a headline, in which case the symbol is placed after its first and only use in the headline. If the first mention of a mark appears in a title, heading, or ad headline, the short version of the identifier on the marks list may be used for readability and conciseness. The paragraph following the head should mention the mark with proper attribution and using its full identifier. If the style of a title, heading, or ad headline is all capital letters, the marks retain their capitalization on the marks list (e.g., initial cap or lowercase).

## 3.3 Figure callouts

Marks in figure callouts are used as adjectives, and the short version of the identifier may be used if space for type is limited.

If the first use of a mark occurs in a figure callout but the mark is also mentioned elsewhere in the document, place the superscript nonregistered trademark symbol on the first mention in the text or table.

If a mark occurs only in a figure callout and nowhere else in the text, but it is not reasonable to either recast the text to include the mark or rewrite the mark in the callout to a generic description, the superscript nonregistered trademark symbol is used for the mark in the callout.

## 3.4 Digital materials

3.4.1 slb.com webpages: SLB marks are addressed in the link “Terms,” which is at the bottom of every public SLB webpage on slb.com. However, still give proper attribution to all

marks discussed in web material. The identifier from the marks list is used per Section 2.1.2.3 in conjunction with the first mention of the mark in the text on a webpage. Use of the short identifier is allowed because of space constraints.

Because web technology facilitates nonlinear access of information, reintroduce the mark and its identifier as possible wherever a reader is most likely to access a digital mention from a link or by using a search engine.

It is recognized that full compliance with principles for first mention of marks (using long identifier) is not possible to achieve in all digital material. It is also recognized that it is difficult to consistently use superscript nonregistered trademark symbols for marks in content that can be used in both digital and printed publications, like software training content. In long technical documentation, such as training guides, the Table of Content can serve as a natural and logical place for the first use of a mark.

3.4.2 Digital ads: Digital ads posted on other websites do use the superscript nonregistered trademark symbol. If it is used in a title or text it can use the short identifier to fit space constraints. If the ad has a large graphic area, an attribution statement can optionally be included on it.

3.4.3 Posted or linked files: Marks in PDFs or other file formats posted to the web are treated as printed documents (see Section 2.1.2).

## 3.4.4 Email: Marks in marketing material that is published as email messages are treated

identically to marks in printed material (see Section 2.1.2).

## 3.5 Slides presentations

Because slides may be rearranged for custom presentations, an attribution note is used to identify SLB marks on each first mention per slide. If marks of other companies are used, they are attributed on a slide that immediately follows the title slide (see Section 2.2): “Other company, product, and service names are the properties of their respective owners.” If no SLB marks are used in the presentation but other companies’ marks are, use “Company, product, and service names are the properties of their respective owners.” Slide templates can be downloaded from “Brand Elements and Templates.”

## 4.0 Copyright Protection and Permissions

This section describes the copyright that applies to materials created by or for SLB and gives guidelines on how to use the copyrighted works of others. It also addresses the permissions required before photographs of people can be used in SLB publications and how to make and respond to requests to reproduce copyrighted materials.

Copyright is a form of intellectual property law that protects original works of authorship— whether unpublished or published in print or electronically or by any other means—as long as they are in a tangible form. Copyright does not protect facts, ideas, systems, or methods of operation, although it may protect the way these things are expressed.

Generally, the owner of a copyright has the exclusive right to protect the copyrighted work from unauthorized use by others. Copyrighted work cannot be reproduced, used as the basis for derivative works, distributed, displayed, or performed without the consent of the owner.

An example of a common copyright violation is cutting and pasting content from one website to another or to another media form without the permission of the copyright owner. Another is scanning a cartoon from the newspaper to include in a newsletter without the permission of the

copyright owner. It is just as much a copyright violation to reproduce the owner’s work for internal SLB purposes as it is to reproduce the work for distribution outside SLB.

## 4.1 SLB copyright identification

All SLB documents identify the copyright owner as SLB; names of Divisions are not included in the copyright line:

Correct: Copyright © [year] SLB. All rights reserved. Use a copyright date range of © SLB xxxx–yyyy for a derivative work (meaning with many iterations over the years), with xxxx the date of the first publication and yyyy the date of the latest release. If the work is an SLB acquisition, the first publishing date remains the date of first publication and is not modified to the date of first SLB publication.

Because of space constraints and their visual design emphasis, advertisements and tradeshow banners and posters may use a shorter statement:

Correct: © [year] SLB. For public SLB webpages on slb.com, the link “Terms” includes the copyright identification.

## 4.2 Copyright ownership of works for hire

The Legal Department has prepared standard contracts for vendors and contractors to ensure that SLB owns the copyrights to works prepared by people such as writers, artists, designers, and photographers. These contracts are available from Human Resources and must be signed by the person performing a work for hire and by the appropriate SLB authority (designated by Human Resources).

## 4.3 Permissions to reproduce copyrighted materials

SLB authors, writers, and editors are typically concerned with two types of permissions: • permission SLB must obtain from a copyright holder before we can legally reproduce copyrighted material • permission SLB must give to others before they can legally reproduce materials copyrighted by SLB.

A request for permission to reproduce material must be in writing (letter or email) and include sufficient information for an informed decision. In turn, the reply to the request must include the scope of the permission granted, the attribution required, and fee, if applicable. The following information should be included in a request to expedite the permission process: • title, date of publication, identification of the original work, and specific information (page number, figure number, webpage address, etc.) on what is to be reproduced • title and information about the publication that will contain the reproduced material (purpose of publication, intended audience and distribution, context that reproduced material will be presented in and, if possible, a copy of the relevant context, such as a book chapter, CD-ROM, or website page) • scope of permission requested (one use of material, unlimited use, translation, etc.).

SLB personnel who receive requests for permission to reproduce SLB materials should direct the requests to the appropriate Division MarCom Manager. If permission is granted to reproduce, the following acknowledgment is required:

This graphic is copyright SLB, Ltd. Used with permission. An alternative acknowledgment typically used in media relations work is the following:

Image courtesy of SLB. SLB has an agreement with the Copyright Clearance Center (CCC) that allows reproduction in printed form of many copyrighted articles and publications without having to seek specific permission to make such copies. The Legal Department should be contacted for specifics of which publications are covered and what is permitted. A major exception to the CCC agreement is SPE papers, which are covered under a separate agreement and for which the reproduction right is subject to several restrictions.

For permission received for SLB to reproduce material, use the attribution requested by the provider as possible.

## 4.3.1 Reproducing without permission (fair use)

The doctrine of fair use provides for a limited use of copyrighted materials without permission for educational, research, and review and reporting purposes but does not define the limits. The law of fair use is complex and subject to many exceptions. To see if an excerpt from a copyrighted work can be used, contact the Legal Department for advice on whether the use would constitute a fair use.

## 4.3.2 Retaining SLB copyright to graphics in technical papers or other

publications Many technical societies require authors to assign the copyright to their papers to the society before the paper can be presented at a conference or symposium or published in a proceedings volume. If such papers contain graphics from SLB communications and marketing materials— including material used externally (brochure, ad, presentation, etc.), internally, and for investor relations (annual report, presentation, etc.)—authors must protect SLB’s right to reprint the material by excluding such figures from the copyright reassignment.

## 4.4 Permissions required for photographs and photo shoots

The following permissions apply to all photographs uploaded to the Media Bank.

4.4.1 For photo shoots that are not on SLB premises, obtain written permission to conduct the shoot from the appropriate authority, such as the client or property owner. Email permissions are also acceptable.

## 4.4.2 Acquire a signed release form from each person photographed, both SLB personnel and

people not employed by SLB.

Overview Our SLB brand personality inspires everything we do and say. When someone has a great personality, we are naturally drawn to them. It’s a trait that makes us want to spend time with another person and what makes them memorable for the right reasons. When it comes to our SLB brand, personality works in the same way. A brand personality is the set of human characteristics and traits that a brand upholds: It makes a company relatable. A strong brand personality can build brand loyalty and equity. It gives our brand an emotional foothold in the mind of our audience.

Your tone and choice of words should give life and meaning to the message to support the SLB brand. As the author of material on slb.com, a technical paper, or other SLB publication, you determine the value, scope, and content of your presentation. You must be able to support your ideas, especially if your assertions contradict accepted scientific knowledge. References and measurements must be accurate; theoretical justifications and examples must be used to substantiate your statements.

Tone is dependent on your audience and the type of marketing collateral being produced: This can range from a more conversational tone to one that is more technical. For technical content, technical accuracy is the backbone of SLB leadership. It bears directly on the impact of the publication and helps uphold the company’s reputation for high-quality technology. Writers must be diligent to ensure that all SLB documents are technically accurate. Technical content is directly influenced by the language used to describe it; therefore, weigh your words as carefully as your data.

Appropriate vocabulary and a logical structure help communicate your message and lend credibility to your ideas. That credibility is weakened by inconsistencies in terminology and style as well as by typographical errors, misspelled words, and incorrect grammar. The best technical message may be lost to the audience in a poor presentation. The following guidelines can help you avoid this pitfall.

These guidelines for print materials and slb.com assume that you already know the basics of effective writing. They are intended to steer you through some of the more obscure details of style and to set the standards that present a consistent SLB image. Consult the SLB Communications Handbook first if you have a question; if your question is not addressed, then check these additional authorities on which SLB style is based: • Merriam-Webster’s Collegiate Dictionary • Oxford Style Manual (2003), comprising two titles previously published as The Oxford Dictionary for Writers and Editors and The Oxford Guide to Style • Society of Petroleum Engineers Publications Style Guide • The Chicago Manual of Style.

Additional resources are listed in the “Suggested reading” section of this document. Guidance for print templates is in the SLB “Brand Elements and Templates.” Guidance for slb.com elements is in “Writing for Our Public Websites.” If you have questions, comments, or suggestions about the “Writing and Style” guidelines, email Victoria Morales at vmorales7@slb.com.

Writing for your audience Most SLB external marketing publications, print and slb.com, are created for a global audience and are distributed internationally. US English is the language used for this audience. The “Writing and Style” guidelines identify style variations for digital and print communications and for local audiences.

For external marketing publications addressing a local audience and distributed only locally or regionally, the appropriate English language variation is used. If you are writing for a local audience, it is best to consult authoritative editorial resources for the area, such as a dictionary and writing and style guides. See resources listed in “Suggested reading” for important issues in communicating with specific audiences. For the best results and the clearest communications, work with a local resource or vendor trained in SLB “Brand Elements and Templates.” Grammar Because sentences are the building blocks of thought, the communication of a message is directly related to the clarity and readability of each sentence. Sentence length is one measure of readability because the reader must read, understand, interpret, and remember information sentence by sentence. Readability studies have shown that sentences of 20 or fewer words are the most easily comprehended.

While shorter sentences are preferred, varying the length of sentences adds interest to your writing. Reserve long sentences for presenting involved concepts and for elaborating on a point that requires some thought. Use short sentences for stating clear, crisp thoughts. Regardless of sentence length, state your point clearly and directly. You can do this by following these rules: • Position the subject as close as possible to the verb. • Place modifiers as close as possible to the words they modify. • Use conjunctions such as and, or, but, and then to show connection, contrast, and sequence.

The most emphatic positions within a sentence are at the beginning or end. Key ideas should have important positions, and less important ideas should be subordinated. Transposing words or phrases changes the emphasis in the following sentences: • Coal mining has been an important industry in this region for three decades.

(Emphasis on coal mining) or • For three decades coal mining has been an important industry in this region.

(Emphasis on time frame) or • In this region coal mining has been an important industry for three decades.

(Emphasis on location) Along with these rules, check your writing for common grammatical errors.

Sentence structure A complete sentence has at least one independent clause (a group of words containing a subject and verb) that stands by itself to express a complete thought. Ending a sentence too soon results in a sentence Incorrect: The well that the company drilled.

(sentence fragment) Incorrect: This is the well that the company drilled, it is 2,745 m deep. (Incorrectly joining two independent clauses produces a run-on sentence.)

fragment; ending it too late results in a run-on sentence. Correct: This is the well that the company drilled; it is 2,745 m deep.

Correct: This is the well that the company drilled, and it is 2,745 m deep.

Correct: This is the well that the company drilled. It is 2,745 m deep.

Agreement between sentence elements The subject of a sentence should agree in number with the sentence verb. Compound subjects and words and phrases between the subject and the verb are common pitfalls in subject-verb agreement. For example, phrases such as with, along with, including, as well as, together with, and plus between the subject and verb do not make a singular verb plural. Collective nouns and pronouns take either a singular or plural verb, depending on whether they mean a unit or separate items, respectively.

UK English: The practice of construing collective nouns either with a singular verb to emphasize unity or with a plural verb to emphasize individuality is well established.

Names of institutions and political entities are always treated as singular.

Incorrect: The number of workstation users have increased twofold.

Correct: The number of workstation users has increased twofold.

Incorrect: The composition and quality of each sample was impressive.

Correct: The composition and quality of each sample were impressive.

Incorrect: The BHA, plus the tools it requires, were packed in the shipping container.

Correct: The BHA, plus the tools it requires, was packed in the shipping container.

Correct: The driller, as well as the customer’s representative, arrives tomorrow.

Correct: Only 3 m [10 ft] of sandstone was drilled before the bit failed.

Pronouns and the nouns they replace must agree in number, gender, and case.

Incorrect: Smaller, more complex fields are being developed. This requires more log data for reservoir evaluation. (This, which is singular, refers to fields, which is plural.)

Correct: Smaller, more complex fields are being developed. These require more log data for reservoir evaluation.

Incorrect: Everyone is responsible for their safety equipment. (his or her?)

Correct: All employees are responsible for their safety equipment.

The antecedent of a pronoun must be unmistakable. (An antecedent is the noun or pronoun to which a following pronoun refers.)

Incorrect: A safety joint is run above the packer. It can be unscrewed to remove the upper part of the string. (The antecedent of the pronoun it is ambiguous.)

Incorrect: A safety joint is run above the packer, which can be unscrewed to remove the upper part of the string.

Correct: A safety joint, which can be unscrewed to remove the upper part of the string, is run above the packer.

Verb tense Present, past, or future action should be described consistently within the same sentence.

Incorrect: The geoscientist processes the data and stored the file on tape.

Correct: The geoscientist processes the data and stores the file on tape.

Use the past tense to state what was done. Incorrect: We tested the core samples and confirm the presence of hydrocarbons.

Correct: We tested the core samples and confirmed the presence of hydrocarbons.

Use the present tense for scientific facts and truths. Incorrect: Water will freeze at 0 degC. (future tense) Correct: Water freezes at 0 degC.

Present and past tenses are used as appropriate for results, discussion, and conclusions.

Correct: Pressure measurements demonstrated that six of the zones consisted of reservoir-quality rock.

Correct: In “Guidelines for Cement Sheath Evaluation,” Goodwin pointed out that almost any solid material in the annulus provides adequate support to the casing.

Correct: The high resolution of vertical seismic profiles enables interpreters to find faults, refine structures, and detect stratigraphic boundaries away from the wellbore.

Use the present tense to distinguish current theory from previous work.

Correct: Jones (1976) argues that the type of material in the annulus must be known to determine whether the zone has good isolation, whereas Smith (1935) believed that the quality of zonal isolation depended solely on the formation characteristics.

Modifiers The most common word order in sentences is subject, verb, and object. Modifiers added to expand and clarify meaning should be placed next to or near the words they modify. The importance of word position is illustrated by how the meaning of the following sentence changes by moving the adverb only.

During perforating only, qualified personnel are allowed on the rig. (Qualified personnel may not go on the rig at any other time.) or During perforating, only qualified personnel are allowed on the rig. (No one else may go on the rig at that time.) or During perforating, qualified personnel are allowed on the rig only. (They may not go anywhere else.)

Incorrect: The core samples were evaluated from Texas. Correct: The core samples from Texas were evaluated.

A participial phrase must logically modify its antecedent. (A participle is a verb that functions as an adjective to modify nouns and pronouns. It usually ends in –ed or –ing. A participial phrase is a participle, adjectives that describe it, and complements that complete the meaning of the phrase.)

Incorrect: Comparing laboratory and field results, it is evident that the gas is prevalent in the zone of interest. (It is unable to make comparisons.)

Correct: Comparing laboratory and field results, we learned that gas is prevalent in the zone of interest.

Incorrect: Because they are highly sensitive to changes in pressure, engineers should specify quartz crystals for the pressure sensors.

Correct: Because they are highly sensitive to changes in pressure, quartz crystals should be specified for the pressure sensors.

Parallel construction Sentence elements that are parallel in meaning should also be parallel in style.

Incorrect: The consultant suggested restoring reservoir pressure and resumption of production.

Correct: The consultant suggested restoring reservoir pressure and resuming production.

Complete comparisons When making a comparison, make sure that the structure of the sentence leads to a comparison of like elements.

Incorrect: These producing wells are similar to the Delaware Basin.

Correct: These producing wells are similar to those in the Delaware Basin.

Verbal identity, tone, and voice Verbal identity What you say and how you say it should be crafted carefully. It reflects SLB. (Reference the “Brand Guidelines”) A clear verbal identity • helps us to stand out, creating differentiation from competitors • communicates our personality and shapes audience perception • builds consistency, trust, and familiarity • helps us sound our best, always.

Our SLB verbal identity incorporates the language—the words, phrases, names, and terms that we use to describe our brand—as well as the tone, voice, style, and messages we use to

communicate. Both internally and externally. Locally and globally. When writing for the web, our verbal identity is made up of four building blocks: • Personality: how we want to be perceived (e.g., bold, bright blue, balanced). Inspirational, not dry. Think solutions, not problems. Uplifting and positive. • Voice: how we talk to the world. Authentic, not corporate. Write for a person, not a product.

Turn up the warmth, connection, and understanding. • Language: how we write. Use simple and clear words. Blatant promotional language is not used. We state facts, not unsubstantiated claims. • Tone: how we modulate our verbal identity. Conversational, not dictatorial. Intelligent, not academic. Connect with the audience and speak to their needs. Draw them into the story, not overwhelm or bore them.

Tone When writing for slb.com and some ads, in general, use a more conversational tone, where appropriate (Reference the “Dialing up or down different aspects of our tone” table): • We and our are substituted for most mentions of SLB. • Readers are often addressed as you, instead of referring to them in the third person. • Contractions are freely used. • Starting sentences with conjunctions is allowed. • Sentence fragments are allowed, within context, to substitute for complete sentences.

Dialing up or down different aspects of our tone to emphasize a particular aspect of our personality.

Personality Tone Language Collateral Bold (leadership and innovation) Confident (not arrogant) Inspirational (not dry) Intelligent (not academic) Concise Optimistic Engaging Human Clear Content in which we clearly have a bold ownership story to tell.

Examples: Thought leadership Customer solutions: case studies and tech reports News and press releases Announcements Strategic messaging Bright blue (personable) Intelligent (not academic) Conversational (not dictatorial) Authentic (not corporate) Concise Optimistic Engaging Human Clear Content and channels where showcasing people and creating dialog is essential. Examples:

Social media: LinkedIn, Instagram, etc. People stories

Careers Brand campaigns Events Balanced (science, impact, and integrity) Confident (not arrogant) Intelligent (not academic) Authentic (not corporate) Concise Optimistic Engaging Human Clear Content that is essential for demonstrating scientific rigor.

Examples: Reports Customer solutions: case studies, tech reports, etc.

Technology Sustainability content Impact statistics and infographics Voice The choice of verbs can make a great difference in the force of your writing. If the subject of the sentence is the agent of the action, the voice is active; if the subject is the recipient of the action, the voice is passive.

Passive voice is generally less emphatic than active voice because the emphasis is shifted to the object acted on rather than the agent of the action. Good writing blends both voices with the active voice predominating.

Passive: The limestone is cut obliquely by fault lines. Active: The fault lines cut the limestone obliquely.

Do not use passive voice to avoid the first person pronouns I, me, we, or us. Although it is sometimes appropriate to avoid mention of the actor, in many cases the passive voice creates ambiguity.

Passive: An experiment was done to show well flowing pressures.

Active: We conducted an experiment to show well flowing pressures.

Three techniques for converting passive to active voice are to reverse the word order in the sentence or clause, change the verb, or reword the sentence.

Passive: These techniques are described in great detail in the new manual.

Active: The new manual describes these techniques in great detail.

Passive: Introduction of these techniques is expected to improve productivity.

Active: Productivity will probably improve when these techniques are introduced.

Spelling Correct spelling reinforces both the clarity and credibility of any document. Misspelled words divert the reader’s attention away from the message and may cause the writer to appear incompetent, careless, and potentially untrustworthy. Therefore, proofread your documents carefully before publishing.

A computerized spelling-check program may help you find spelling errors in electronic copy; however, spelling checkers should not be substituted for proofreading because they cannot account for meaning and context. For example, a spelling checker will not find an instance where the word from is typed instead of form. Also, some programs pass over words that are not in the program dictionary.

Challenge the spelling of every word in your document. Oilfield and technical terms, acronyms, and other words are in the list of “Spellings and Terminology.” If a word is not listed there, consult Merriam-Webster’s Collegiate Dictionary (11th ed.), which is one of the most widely accepted authorities for spelling. The first listed spelling of a word is preferred. “Suggested reading” lists geoscience glossaries, dictionaries, and other resources that can be consulted.

American spelling (US English) is used for slb.com and all SLB documents that have an international distribution or audience (except for proper names of people, companies, and institutions and for direct quotations). UK English or other alternatives can be used for external marketing materials that will be distributed only locally or regionally outside the United States. If you are writing for a local audience, consult an authoritative resource or dictionary for the area to ensure that your spellings are correct.

Compound words To determine whether a compound word should be closed (backream), hyphenated (back-ream), or open (back ream), first check the list of “Spellings and Terminology.” The format for various prefixes and suffixes is also on the list. If the compound word is not on the list, then consult Webster’s. If the word is not in Webster’s, then consult The Chicago Manual of Style, particularly the hyphenation guide in Section 7.85. Open compounds that are well-known terms are usually not hyphenated when they are used as adjectives before the modified noun. If the compound adjective comes after the modified noun, an added hyphen is necessary only to avoid ambiguity.

Special characters, symbols, and word formats For Microsoft® Office documents, symbols and special characters should be inserted with American National Standards Institute (ANSI) codes because they are more stable than those inserted by using the Insert menu or keyboard shortcuts. To insert an ANSI symbol or character code, NUM LOCK must be on. Hold down the ALT key and use the numeric keypad to type in the four-numeral code. Similar character codes are used for Macintosh® computers.

Letters that represent shapes (for example, O-ring) are capitalized and set in roman type.

Italics Italics can be used to flag a word, such as a key term on its first mention or definition, but they should be used only sparingly for emphasis in technical writing.

Foreign words and phrases are set in italics only if they are not familiar. However, if an unfamiliar foreign word is used repeatedly throughout a work, it need be italicized only on its first occurrence. Familiar foreign words and phrases (for example, a priori, résumé) are listed in the English language section of Webster’s and are set in roman type.

The names of specific ships or other such vessels are in italics, but the name of a make or class is not (for example, The DeepSTIM II offshore stimulation vessel is the flagship of the DeepSTIM™ fleet). The only use of italics on slb.com is for names of specific ships and vessels.

Quotation marks can also be used to identify a special term on its first mention. Do not use both quotation marks and italics for this purpose and maintain consistency of use within the same document by not mixing the two.

Foreign words Foreign words and phrases familiar to most readers and listed in Webster’s are not italicized if used in an English context; they should be spelled as in that dictionary. German nouns that are in Webster’s are lowercased. If following these guidelines could cause confusion, it is best to italicize, spell, and capitalize foreign terms as they are in the original language. If a familiar foreign term occurs in the same context as a less familiar one, maintain consistency by italicizing both or neither.

Word choice and usage Good writers make all words, sentences, and paragraphs count by removing unnecessary words without sacrificing clarity. They avoid using excessive adjectives, adverbs, and intensifier words such as very and quite. Effective writers also achieve conciseness by eliminating undesirable repetition in their text. Check your writing for the following examples of wordiness.

Choosing the right words Avoid using complex words when direct, natural words or phrases are available. Words with many syllables slow the reader and may sound pretentious or stilted.

Instead of Use along the lines of like assistance aid, help at present, at this point in time now due to the fact that, in view of the fact that because employed used facilitate help, ease foregoing this, these furthermore also have to, need to must implement use in advance of before inasmuch as because in case of if initiate begin in order to to in spite of the fact that although in the event that if in the final analysis finally in the foreseeable future soon in the majority of instances usually in the vicinity of, in close proximity to near ought to should with regard to, with reference to about, concerning with the exception of except

Avoid timeworn clichés and jargon. Be sure the terms you use are readily understood by your reader. If your document will be distributed to different groups (for example, engineers and geologists), avoid the jargon of either group.

Instead of Use quick as a flash quickly last but not least finally as plain as day obvious step change improvement game changer revolutionary Eliminate redundancy.

Instead of Use actual measurement measurement bisect into two parts bisect close proximity proximity completely absent absent dropped down dropped final conclusion conclusion first began began gray in color gray join together join low-lying depression depression mix together mix period of time period, time refer back refer rise up rise surrounded on all sides surrounded throughout the entire throughout Avoid words that can have a triggering effect for external audiences.

Potentially emotive word or phrase Alternative wording environmentally friendly; environmentally acceptable ecologically sound; environmentally benign green (unless in the context of an official classification, such as "green chemical" official label in Norway) ecologically sound costly; expensive cost prohibitive (Technology benefits should be priority, e.g., increased production; improved efficiency; reduced capex; reduced opex; reduced cost; reduced risk) safe, safely (For liability reasons, avoid these absolutes. “Safer” is generally okay when comparing with a conventional approach) reliable, reliably, secure

Choose the correct word. The following words are frequently misused.

Ability, capacity, able, capable. Ability is the power to do something. Capacity is the power to contain or receive. Able indicates the possession of ability, whereas capable means having adequate capacity to do, make, or receive action.

Incorrect: The capacity to successfully complete the fracturing operation depended on the ability of the tank.

Correct: The ability to successfully complete the fracturing operation depended on the capacity of the tank.

Correct: The tool is able to log rugose boreholes. Correct: The tool is capable of logging rugose boreholes.

About, around. Both about and around are used to describe a physical position, but only about is used to mean approximately.

Correct: The engineer walked about the rig to inspect it. Correct: The engineer walked around the rig to inspect it.

Incorrect: Around 90 samples were received. Correct: About 90 samples were retrieved.

Accuracy, precision. Accuracy refers to the correctness of a measured or computed value or a statement; precision is used for values reported in very small increments and describes the degree of coincidence (repeatability and reproducibility) of repeated measurements.

Correct: We checked the manufacturer’s documentation to confirm the accuracy of our field measurement.

Correct: The series of identical measurements was precise; however, the value of 5.42 g/cm3 was not necessarily more accurate than the previously determined value of 5.4 g/cm3.

Actually, basically (see “essential, essentially”), literally. Actually and literally mean truly but are usually not necessary as descriptors. Basically means fundamentally; avoid its usage as chiefly or for the most part.

Incorrect: The technology actually increases profits in most new applications.

Correct: Despite its touted efficacy, the technology actually increased application time.

Incorrect: Market growth basically follows technology growth.

Correct: Market growth mainly follows technology growth. Acute, chronic. Acute means of great importance, intense, or having a rapid onset followed by a short course, which is the opposite of the meaning of chronic as long- lasting or recurrent. The use of chronic for habitual has negative connotations.

Correct: The acute lack of funding stalled the development project.

Incorrect: Chronic feedback improved the new hires' performance.

Correct: Constant feedback improved the new hires' performance.

Affect, effect. Affect (verb) means to influence. Effect (noun) is the result, and effect (verb) means to achieve or bring about a change.

Allow, enable, permit. Allow means to let have and implies no restraint. Enable means to give an ability. Permit specifically involves giving permission.

Alternate, alternative. Alternate means one after the other. Alternative means one or the other.

Although, whereas. Although means regardless of the fact that. Whereas means while on the contrary and implies a clear contrast between two statements.

Among, between. Use among when referring to three or more items and between when referring to two. Between can be used for more than two items if each item has an individual relation to the others.

Incorrect: The maps were distributed among the driller and the engineer.

Correct: The maps were distributed between the driller and the engineer.

Correct: The maps were distributed among the driller, engineer, and geologist.

Correct: Obtaining understanding between oil-producing countries is desired.

And, or. Avoid the slashed use of and/or. Either word may be sufficient; the words or both may be added.

Incorrect: The bedding planes may include shale and/or sandstone.

Incorrect: The bedding planes may include shale/sandstone. Correct: The bedding planes may include shale or sandstone or both.

Correct: The bedding planes may include shale, sandstone, or both.

As much as, up to. As much as is preferred for describing an upper limit. Up to implies a position in space.

As, since, because. As is often imprecise when used as a conjunction indicating cause.

Choose the precise word: while, when, because, or since. When since or because introduce a dependent clause, because always denotes direct cause and since often connotes time and may imply cause, reason, or result.

Incorrect: Since the FMI™ fullbore formation microimager tool provides images of the borehole wall, thin events can be detected.

Correct: Because the FMI™ fullbore formation microimager tool provides images of the borehole wall, thin events can be detected.

Incorrect: As the software was installed, the speed of data interpretation has tripled.

Correct: Since the software was installed, the speed of data interpretation has tripled.

Assure, ensure, insure. Assure means to encourage. Ensure means to make certain.

Insure is used to refer to underwriting a loss. Based on, on the basis of. Based on modifies the noun in the main clause of the sentence; on the basis of modifies the verb.

Incorrect: Based on the measurements, researchers estimate . . . (Researchers are not based on the measurements; the estimates are.)

Correct: Researchers’ estimates were based on measurements . . .

Can, may. Can suggests ability; may expresses permission or possibility.

Correct: We can evaluate the formation with the new tool. (It is feasible.)

Correct: We may evaluate the formation with the tool. (implies that chances are likely that it will be evaluated or that permission will be granted) Client, customer. Use customer, not client, for business relationships.

Correct: Downhole test data was streamed in real time to the customer’s office.

Cloud: Use in the cloud for cloud computing. Incorrect: Access your data on the Cloud.

Correct: Access your data in the cloud. Compare to, compare with. Compare to implies resemblances between different ideas or things (you and an elephant). Compare with implies contrasts between similar things (you and me).

Use similar instead of comparable to indicate likeness. Correct: The firing of a seismic shot has been compared to a mild earthquake.

Correct: Winter weather in the Gulf of Mexico is calm compared with that of the North Sea.

Incorrect: The high rate of data transmission from the LWD tool is comparable to that of conventional wireline tools.

Correct: The high rate of data transmission from the LWD tool is similar to that of conventional wireline tools.

Competitor, third party, or conventional We don't acknowledge that we have competitors. Instead of “competitor,” mention “third-party company” or “conventional technology.” Incorrect:

Competitor company Competitor drill bit Correct: Third-party company Conventional drill bit Complement, compliment. Complement means to complete or make whole.

Compliment means to praise or admire. Compose, comprise, consist, constitute, include. It takes A, B, and C to compose or constitute a whole, but the whole comprises A, B, and C. Avoid is comprised of. Include indicates an incomplete listing of constituents.

Incorrect: Four companies comprise the joint venture. Correct: The joint venture comprises four companies.

Correct: The outcrop includes limestone and sandstone. (implies that other constituents may also be present) Conspicuous, prominent. These words may both mean immediately noticeable, but prominent suggests a physical jutting out.

Rock colors may be conspicuous, but a salt dome may be both conspicuous and prominent.

Continual, continuous. Continual relates to time and implies unceasingly recurrent activity. Continuous refers to uninterrupted action in time or unbroken extent in space.

Data, facts, information. Data generally refers to organized information used to reach conclusions or verify facts. Information is knowledge acquired through study, experience, or hearsay and has a more general connotation than data.

Data can be used as a singular (for references to the entire data collection as one unit) or plural (for references to individual results) noun with the corresponding verb form.

Correct: Is the field data for last month available? Correct: The data from the latest perforating tests are compared with the previous results.

Correct: Monitoring data from all wells in the field were streamed over one connection.

Digitization, digitalization. Digitization is the process of changing from analog to digital form. Digitalization is the use of digital technologies to change a business model and provide new revenue and value-producing opportunities.

Due to. Due to is correctly used to modify a noun or pronoun that directly precedes it or following a form of the verb to be. Avoid its use as an adverb and use since, through, because of, caused by, owing to, or resulting from instead.

Incorrect: We were unable to obtain samples due to the collapsed wellbore.

Correct: We were unable to obtain samples because of the collapsed wellbore.

Correct: Delays due to bad weather are common in North Sea operations.

Correct: Cancellation of the well test was due to the weather. e.g., i.e. E.g. means for example; i.e. means that is. (See guidance on using commas with i.e. and e.g.)

Employ, use, utilize. These words are often interchangeable. Utilize implies a practical or profitable use, and employ also means to hire. Both are more stilted than use.

Entitled, titled. Entitled means the right to do or have something. Titled means to have a title.

Incorrect: He was titled to the promotion because he wrote the report entitled “Downhole Testing Safety.” Correct: He was entitled to the promotion because he wrote the report titled “Downhole Testing Safety.” Essential, essentially. Both words mean part of the nature or essence, or a basic property.

Do not substitute these words for such words as almost, chiefly, mainly, virtually, or principally.

etc. Etc. is an abbreviation of the Latin et cetera and means and so forth. Etc. at the end of a list implies that the list is not complete, so it cannot be used with a specific list introduced by for example or such as.

Incorrect: Downhole measurements provide petrophysical information (e.g., rock properties, detailed lithology, etc.).

Correct: Downhole measurements provide petrophysical information (e.g., rock properties and detailed lithology).

Correct: Rock properties, detailed lithology, etc., are petrophysical information provided by downhole measurements.

Farther, further. Use farther when distance is implied and further when referring to time or quantity.

Forego, forgo. Forego means to precede. Forgo means to do without.

If, when, where, whether. If implies uncertainty or a conditional relation. When implies certainty and is used for situations requiring preparation or the passage of time.

Where is related to place and is not a substitute for when, that, for which, in which, or on which. Whether implies alternatives.

Whether or not means regardless of whether and is usually used to emphasize doubt; the phrase should be avoided in technical writing.

Incorrect: Whether he leaves this job, he will not be eligible for severance pay.

Correct: If he leaves this job, he will not be eligible for severance pay.

Incorrect: The log will not be representative when the wrong inputs are selected.

Correct: The log will not be representative if the wrong inputs are selected.

Incorrect: I read in the report where a multizone completion was recommended.

Correct: I read in the report that a multizone completion was recommended.

Incorrect: To learn if the equipment is available, check the inventory list.

Correct: To learn whether the equipment is available, check the inventory list.

Incorrect: The engineer was not sure whether or not to rerun the log.

Correct: The engineer was not sure whether to rerun the log. Intermittent, occasional, sporadic.

Intermittent means starting and stopping at intervals. Occasional and sporadic imply more random occurrences.

Its, it’s. Its is the possessive form of it. It’s is the contraction of it is.

Less, fewer. Less refers to quantity. Fewer refers to number and is generally used with items that can be counted.

Correct: less material Correct: fewer drill collars Lie, lay. The intransitive verb lie means to recline or be situated and takes no direct Incorrect: The limestone lays on the shale.

Correct: The limestone lies on the shale.

object. Its forms are lie, lying, lay, and lain. The transitive verb lay means to put or place and requires an object. Its forms are lay, laying, and laid.

Incorrect: Lie the tool on the catwalk. Correct: Lay the tool on the catwalk.

Like, as. Use like for a conjunction with noun phrases (a noun phrase is a noun or pronoun and any modifiers); use as, as if, or as though for comparing clauses.

Correct: The formation absorbed the treatment like a sponge. Incorrect: Flow from the formation decreased like a choke had been applied.

Correct: Flow from the formation decreased as if a choke had been applied.

Like, such as. A like comparison refers to a person or thing as similar to a group of people or things but excludes that person or thing from the group being discussed. A such as comparison typically includes persons or things as part of the group being discussed.

Incorrect: Building a new deepwater platform like Troll A would not be possible today because of changes in legislation.

Correct: Oil from aging deepwater developments such as Troll and Ekofisk continues to form a substantial portion of world production.

Master, slave. Avoid this configuration terminology that could be interpreted as discriminatory.
