Incorrect: Signals from the master controller synchronize the slave controllers.

Correct: Signals from the main controller synchronize the subsidiary controllers.

Majority, most. Majority means the greater part of something. Most is preferred for indicating greater degree, number, quantity, or size.

Number of. A number of is ambiguous and connotes several. Use the exact number if it is known. About or approximately may be used to show uncertainty.

Incorrect: A number of productive zones were perforated. Correct: Three productive zones were perforated.

Oral, verbal. Verbal refers to what is written, but it also can mean spoken. Oral means spoken only. Ambiguity can be avoided by using written or a similar term instead of verbal.

Incorrect: The publication explained the problem orally and mathematically.

Correct: The publication explained the problem verbally and mathematically.

Correct: The publication explained the problem in words and mathematically.

Over, under. Avoid using these words as the equivalent of phrases such as more than and less than or greater than and fewer than, respectively, if they cause confusion about physical position.

Incorrect: The producing zone dips under over 30 m [100 ft] of massive shale.

Correct: The producing zone dips beneath more than 30 m [100 ft] of massive shale.

Percent, percentage. Percent is used with a spelled-out number (the percent symbol % is used as the unit symbol with measured numerical values). Percentage is used to express a proportion.

Incorrect: 20 percentage of the class Correct: 20% of the class Correct (beginning a sentence): Twenty percent of the class Incorrect: a large percent of the samples Correct: a large percentage of the samples

Presence, present. These words are often superfluous. Presently does not mean at present; it means soon.

Incorrect: The metallic minerals present in the sample . . . Correct: The metallic minerals in the sample . . .

Principal, principle. Principal (noun) means chief person or thing; principal (adjective) means first in rank or importance. Principle is a law, fundamental truth, or doctrine.

Correct: The principal investor in the venture is a person of principle.

Proved, proven. Use proved for the past tense and past participle of the verb prove.

Use proven as an adjective. Incorrect: A leak in the seal was proven responsible for failure of the pump.

Correct: A leak in the seal was proved responsible for failure of the pump.

Incorrect: The pump has a proved record of reliability. Correct: The pump has a proven record of reliability.

Quite, rather, somewhat. Avoid these vague descriptors. Significant. The word means important or to have meaning. It is often misused to mean large.

Incorrect: The oil contained significant asphaltene content. Correct: The oil had a high asphaltene content.

So, so that. So may be used alone to join clauses of equivalent rank. When used to introduce a clause that gives a reason for an action, so that should be used.

Correct: The results were inconclusive, so more tests were required.

Correct: We described the results in detail so that everyone would understand them.

That, which. That distinguishes its subject from all others, whereas which adds detail about the subject. That introduces restrictive (defining) clauses; without these clauses, the essential meaning of the sentence is lost.

Which introduces nonrestrictive (parenthetical) clauses; the sentence still makes sense if the clause is deleted. A clause beginning with which is set off with commas.

(See guidance on using commas with nonrestrictive clauses.) Correct: The Senate reached a new agreement that will provide price incentives to find and develop more gas reserves.

Correct: This region, which is the largest, contains the principal reservoir.

This, that, these, those. These demonstrative pronouns must point directly to their antecedents and should not be used alone if what they refer to is in doubt.

This and its plural, these, are used for references near in time, space, or thought.

That and those are for references that are not so close. Incorrect: The treatment required to improve production involved reservoir analysis. This was an expensive proposition. (Is the antecedent of the pronoun this the treatment as a whole or the analysis component?)

Toward, towards. In US English, the preferred form is without the s.

In UK English the s is used. The same is true for other directional words, such as upward, downward, forward, backward, and afterward.

Correct for US: The bit was guided toward the target bed. Correct for UK: The bit was guided towards the target bed.

Unique. Unless you are certain it is one of a kind, use unusual or rare. Do not use unique with qualifiers such as very, rather, or quite.

Use, utilize. Utilize suggests a new or practical use for something. Do not use it routinely for use.

Incorrect: The wrench was utilized to tighten the bolt. Correct: The wrench was used to tighten the bolt.

Correct: Because the nearest medical facility was a half day’s drive, a wrench was utilized as a splint to set the broken leg.

Varied, various. Varied means altered or changed. Various means different or diverse.

Incorrect: Deposition of varied types of scale deposits resulted from the various rate of production.

Correct: Deposition of various types of scale deposits resulted from the varied rate of production.

Via is not restricted to meaning by way of in a geographical sense. It can be used as by means of, such as for conveyance or to describe electronic communications.

Correct: The tool was returned from the base via truck. Correct: The tool was returned from the base via Kuala Lumpur.

Correct: The well report was sent via e-mail. While, when. Both while and when may be used to introduce a contrast, but when has a sense of although. While implies contemporaneity (at the time that).

Who, whom. Use who when the pronoun is the subject of the verb that follows and whom when it is the object of the verb.

Incorrect: The supervisor determined whom would write the report.

Correct: The supervisor determined who would write the report.

Correct: The committee would not reveal whom they nominated to be officers.

Gender-neutral language Words are preferably not gender specific.

Instead of Use chairman chair fireman, policeman firefighter, police officer layman laypeople, layperson, lay public, nonprofessionals man (verb) staff, cover, operate, run, work

Instead of Use manhole access or access hole, entry or entry hole, utility hole man-hour staff-hour, labor-hour, labor time, work-hour mankind humanity, humankind, people manmade artificial, fabricated, manufactured, synthetic, constructed manpower staff, workforce, personnel, employees, workers Routine use of the pronouns he and his is usually inaccurate. The numerous ways of achieving gender-neutral language are demonstrated for the following sentence: The lead engineer should familiarize himself with the well data before beginning his job design. (The noun engineer has no implied gender.)

Change singular forms to plural (they and theirs). Incorrect: The lead engineer should familiarize themselves with the well data before beginning their job design. (singular antecedent and plural pronouns) Correct: The lead engineers should familiarize themselves with the well data before beginning their job designs.

Change to the first person (for example, I, we, us, our) or to the second person (for example, you, yourself).

Correct: As the lead engineer, I should familiarize myself with the well data before beginning my job design.

Correct: If you are the lead engineer, you should familiarize yourself with the well data before beginning your job design.

Use the imperative mood. Correct: Be familiar with the well data before beginning the job design.

Eliminate the pronouns. Correct: The lead engineer should be familiar with the well data before beginning the job design.

Correct: The well data should be familiar to the lead engineer before the job design is begun.

Use he or she and his or her sparingly. Correct: The lead engineer should familiarize him- or herself with the well data before beginning his or her job design.

Similarly, gender-specific pronouns are not used to personify inanimate objects or ideas because English does not assign grammatical gender. For example, do not use she for ships or natural phenomena.

Names of people, places, and things People The use and position of surnames, family names, and given names are not the same in all languages and cultures. Check an authority, such as The Chicago Manual of Style or Oxford Style

Manual, when using non-English personal names (French, Russian, Arabic, Chinese, etc.) in an English-language context.

Initials: Close up initials in a person’s name. Incorrect: P. D. Smith Correct: P.D. Smith Correct: W.N.T. Doran Titles or rank or position: Capitalize titles that precede a name. Do not capitalize titles that follow a name. Titles used without the name or generically are usually lowercased.

Exceptions include displayed lists of names and titles and certain promotional materials.

Correct: George Wilson, field engineer, began his career with SLB in 1954.

Correct: Field Engineer George Wilson began his career with SLB in 1954.

Correct: The field engineer began his career in 1954. Signatures: Individuals’ signatures are not used in publications or on the Web because of security concerns. Exceptions are as required by law or regulation.

Places US states and territories and Canadian provinces and territories: Names of the states and territories of the United States and provinces and territories of Canada are normally spelled out in text.

Incorrect: The field office moved to Laramie, WY. Correct: The field office moved to Laramie, Wyoming.

Correct: Kingston, Ontario Countries and country codes: The ISO maintains the official and most up-to-date list of countries. Refer to ISO 3166 for official spellings. For space-constrained uses, use the options available on this platform.

Correct: France Correct: FR (Alpha-2 code) Correct: FRA (Alpha-3 code) the Netherlands: A lowercase definite article is used except in display text, such as stacked address lines, lists of contributors, and references.

Correct: Production from tight gas plays in the Netherlands is growing.

Correct: Operators International

## 29 Production Row

## 17 AJ Amsterdam

The Netherlands Correct: LN Homer, Consultant, The Netherlands

Cities: Except for the listed cities, which are well known in the oil industry, follow the name of a city with the state (in the United States) or province (in Canada) and the country on first mention. Exceptions are references and listings that require parallel formatting, such author affiliations that each include the city, state or province, and country.

Use commas to separate the parts of addresses and names of geographical places or political divisions.

English names for foreign cities should be used (for example, Rome instead of Roma and Belgrade instead of Beograd). If a city is renamed, place names that incorporate the old name are not automatically renamed; however, Bombay was changed to Mumbai and Bombay High field is now called Mumbai High field.

Cities that can be used alone: Aberdeen London Amsterdam Los Angeles Anchorage Mexico City Athens Moscow Beijing Mumbai Berlin New Orleans Buenos Aires New York City Cairo Paris Calcutta Riyadh Calgary Rio de Janeiro Caracas Rome Chicago San Francisco Dallas Shanghai Denver Singapore Doha Stavanger Dubai Sydney Hong Kong The Hague Houston Tokyo Jakarta Tulsa Kuala Lumpur Washington, DC Incorrect: Data from the Jene field in South Sumatra Indonesia were submitted to the US Geological Survey’s National Center in Reston Virginia and the customer’s office in Houston, Texas.

Correct: Data from the Jene field in South Sumatra, Indonesia, were submitted to the US Geological Survey’s National Center in Reston, Virginia, and the customer’s office in Houston.

Compass points: Compass points and terms that indicate direction or location are lowercased. Regional terms that are proper names are capitalized. Abbreviations are allowed to describe fault orientation.

UK English: Compass points used as spelled- out terms are hyphenated, except for wind directions.

Correct: The beds strike east–west and dip 45° to the south. Correct: the Middle East Correct: a southwest wind Correct: NNW–SSE Correct for US: Geologists identified a fault in the northeast part of the field.

Correct for UK: Geologists identified a fault in the north-east part of the field.

Geographic or generic terms in place names: Capitalize geographic or generic terms if they are part of place names. Capitalize descriptive terms that denote a defined region.

Do not capitalize generic terms when used alone or descriptively.

Correct: Trinity River Correct: Trinity and Sabine Rivers Correct: US Continental Shelf (subsequent mentions can be just “Shelf”) Correct: Mounts McKinley and Olympus Correct: West Coast Correct: western Harris County Correct: the valley of the Mississippi River Incorrect: the Rio Grande River Correct: the Rio Grande

Non-English terms in place names: When a foreign generic term forms part of a geographic name, the English term should not be included.

Foreign names of thoroughfares and buildings are not italicized and may be preceded by the English word the if the definite article is used in the original language. Do not use both the foreign and English terms.

Incorrect: the Le Champ-Elysées Correct: the Champs-Elysées Incorrect: The SLB office is on rue St.

Dominique Street. Correct: The SLB office is on rue St. Dominique.

Geologic place names: Do not capitalize descriptive uses of words such as basin, belt, delta, field, pay, play, pool, reservoir, sand, shale, and trend. They are capitalized as part of a proper name.

Correct: San Juan Basin Correct: A famous East Texas field is Luling Field Correct: These wells in the Eagle Ford Play target the Eagle Ford Shale and Woodbine Formation.

Things Companies, institutions, buildings, thoroughfares, and organizations: Capitalize the names of companies, institutions, buildings, thoroughfares, and organizations. Capitalize Geounit and Technology (external) or Business Line (internal) for SLB entities.

Exceptions are names that begin with a lowercase letter. Generic mentions are lowercased.

Foreign names of thoroughfares and buildings are not italicized and may be preceded by the English word the if the definite article is used in the original language. Do not use both the foreign and English terms.

Correct: Gulf Coast Division Incorrect: the Division Correct: the division Correct: The EUR Geounit is headquartered in Aberdeen.

Correct: eBay Correct: the Champs-Elysées Geologic unit names: Capitalize both parts of the name of a lithostratigraphic unit that is formally named in accordance with a recognized classification code. The first part of the name is usually a place name; the second part indicates the unit or stratigraphic rank (for example, supergroup, group, formation, member, bed, supersuite, suite, complex). Unit names for the United States, Canada, and Mexico can be researched at the National Geologic Map Database. For informally named units, which are not named in accordance with a recognized classification code, lowercase the rank or unit part of the name, which should not be a formal stratigraphic rank term.

Correct: The source rocks of Jonah field are probably the coal layers in the lower Lance Formation and upper part of the Mesaverde Group.

Correct: The Barnett Formation in the field consists primarily of 100-ft-thick Barnett Shale with intercalated lenses of Barnett sand.

Casing and tubing grades: Grades are set solid. Common grades from API Spec 5CT:

## C90, C110 N80

## H40 P110

## J55 R95

## K55 T95

## L80 Q125

M65 Ships or other vessels: The names of specific ships or other vessels are in italics, but the name of a make or class is not.

Correct: The DeepStim II offshore stimulation vessel is the flagship of the DeepSTIM™ fleet.

Trademarks: Trademarks must be properly formatted, identified with the appropriate trademark symbol, and used as adjectives only, never as nouns or verbs. A generic term typically can be used instead of a trademarked name. For more information on trademark usage, see the “Legal and Marketing” chapter of the Communications Handbook.

Trademark symbols are not used on slb.com pages. Incorrect: The surface was Teflon.

Correct: The surface had a Teflon® coating. Correct for slb.com pages: The surface had a Teflon coating.

Correct: The surface was coated with polytetrafluoroethylene (PTFE).

Nonmarked or generic SLB offerings: Do not capitalize nonmarked or generic SLB service offerings in body copy. If you want to add a level of importance in web copy, you can bold the name.

Incorrect: Intelligent Power Management is a predictive power management system that reduces emissions, fuel consumption, and engine run time.

Correct: Intelligent power management is a predictive power management system that reduces emissions, fuel consumption, and engine run time.

Correct: Intelligent power management is a predictive power management system that reduces emissions, fuel consumption, and engine run time.

Cloud computing: Application software and data storage are described as in the cloud.

Incorrect: Users access their data on the Cloud. Correct: Users access their data in the cloud.

Uniform resource locators (URLs) and Web addresses: Promotional materials can use a short redirect form that is more easily remembered. Include the full server name and the protocol as necessary in a URL or Web address.

References include full URLs (see guidance on formatting electronic references).

Lowercase all portions of the short URL with the exception of marks and some acronyms.

For URLs that include marks, capitalize the short URL ending as the mark appears in the marks list.

Correct: slb.com Correct: onesubsea.slb.com/subsea-boosting Correct: slb.com/ADD (for “autonomous directional drilling”) Correct: slb.com/PowerDriveArcher Correct: slb.com/PowerDrive-Archer Correct: slb.com/QuantaGeo Correct: slb.com/Quanta-Geo Incorrect: slb.com/quantageo Incorrect: slb.com/Quanta_Geo

Do not use underscores (_) in short URLs. You can choose to use a hyphen or remove the space, whichever is more legible.

If a mark is very long, you may choose to condense the mark only for the short URL.

Correct: slb.com/QGeo (for Quanta Geo) Correct: slb.com/NATCODF (for NATCO

## DUAL FREQUENCY)

Parts of a publication: Capitalize Appendix, Chapter, Equation, Fig., Figure, Section, and Table for specific numbered, lettered, or named components of a work. Lowercase these terms referencing other works to differentiate them.

Correct: The data plotted in Fig. 4 of the following chapter contrast with Waxman and Smit’s (1968) fig. 2.

Punctuation Period A period (also called a full stop or full point in UK English) is used as terminal punctuation for a complete declarative or imperative sentence.

Place a period inside parentheses enclosing a complete and independent sentence. When a complete sentence enclosed by parentheses is within another sentence, it does not receive a period.

If a sentence ends with an abbreviation that ends with a period, that period also serves as the terminal punctuation for the entire sentence.

Correct: Cemented casing with selective perforation (a 7-in casing is recommended) is the prescribed completion.

Correct: Cemented casing with selective perforation is the prescribed completion. (A 7-in casing is recommended.)

Incorrect: The total production rate of 460 m3/d during drawdown came from a fraction of the openhole interval. (50 m) Correct: The total production rate of 460 m3/d during drawdown came from a fraction of the openhole interval (50 m).

Incorrect: Sunrise was at 5 a.m.. Correct: Sunrise was at 5 a.m.

Incorrect: Specifications are at slb.com/oilfield Correct: Specifications are at slb.com/oilfield.

Correct: Request more information on stimulation from stimulation@slb.com.

UK English: A full stop is used to separate the day of the week, month, and year in dates presented numerically in day-month-year format.

UK English: A full stop separates the hour and minutes when 24-h time is not used.

Correct for UK: 16.7.42 (16 July 1942) Correct for UK: 9.15 p.m.

Use of a period as terminal punctuation for titles and headings varies with the context and layout template. Such usage is common in Typical for ad: New depths. New horizons.

Typical for report: Applications in Wells with Low Water Cut

advertisement headlines but infrequent in other headings. A period is used as terminal punctuation for all figure captions, regardless of whether they are complete sentences.

A period is not used as terminal punctuation for a callout, key, or figure title (defined in figure terminology) on a figure unless the element is a complete sentence.

Incorrect: Figure 3. Job design flowchart Correct: Figure 3. Job design flowchart.

Omit the period in abbreviations of foreign- language terms that are contractions.

Correct: Cie (French “Compagnie”) Correct: LP (limited partnership) Correct: PLC (public limited company) Always place periods inside footnote reference numbers.

Always place periods outside superscript trademark symbols or subscripts that are part of a term.

The period follows the superscript nonregistered trademark symbol identifying the first use of a SLB mark that is a company slogan (which is the only kind of mark not used exclusively as an adjective but stands alone) at the end of a sentence. The same arrangement applies to other companies’ slogan marks.

Incorrect: The nonlinear rise in the inertial resistance has long been recognized13.

Correct: The nonlinear rise in the inertial resistance has long been recognized.13 Incorrect: The volume of fluid withdrawn during a single test may be adjusted to a maximum volume of 100 cm.3 Correct: The volume of fluid withdrawn during a single test may be adjusted to a maximum volume of 100 cm3.

Correct: Production success depends on Connecting you to your reservoir™.

Correct: Although the laptop fell through the balcony railing to the sidewalk below, the only damage was a scratch on the logo decal proclaiming Intel Inside®.

Always place periods inside single or double quotation marks.

UK English: If the full stop is not part of the term set off by quotation marks, place it outside the closing single or double quotation marks.

Correct for US: The years between 1865 and 1985 may be called “the century of exploration.” Correct for UK: The years between 1865 and 1985 may be called ‘the century of exploration’.

Question mark and exclamation point A question mark marks a query or expresses uncertainty about an element; it is not used extensively in technical writing.

An exclamation point provides emphasis at the end of a sentence. It is only rarely used in technical writing.

Question marks and exclamation points are used sparingly as necessary in titles and headings.

Correct: What is Value-Added Development? Incorrect: New Acid Diverter Technology!

Correct: New Acid Diverter Technology Question marks and exclamation points are placed within single or double quotation marks Incorrect: She asked, “Why”?

Correct: She asked, “Why?”

or within parentheses only if they are part of the quoted or parenthetical material.

Incorrect: Who initialed this form with an “X?” Correct: Who initialed this form with an “X”?

Comma Place a comma between independent clauses that are joined by coordinating or correlative conjunctions. The comma may be omitted if the clauses are short and closely related.

Two or more verbs that have the same subject are not separated by a comma. If a comma may be necessary to prevent misreading in an extremely complex sentence in which two or more verbs have the same subject, it is preferable to rewrite the sentence for clarity.

(Coordinating conjunctions join clauses of equal status—for example, and, but, for, nor, or, so, yet. Correlative conjunctions are used in pairs to correlate two parts of a sentence—for example, either . . . or, neither . . . nor, both . . . and, not only . . . but also.)

Correct: The position of the bottom boundary was fixed at 439 m, and the top boundary location was adjusted to 431 m.

Correct: The dual packers isolated the interval and the sample was collected. or The dual packers isolated the interval, and the sample was collected.

Incorrect: The transient surge of fluid cleans the perforation damage, and yields a perforation damage skin of zero.

Correct: The transient surge of fluid cleans the perforation damage and yields a perforation damage skin of zero.

Correct: Movement of the tool was adversely affected by heave and cable stretch, and was recorded as flagged values for later correction. or Movement of the tool was adversely affected by heave and cable stretch. The recorded values were flagged for later correction.

Correct: The driller’s assistant was injured when the still-inflated packer element was removed from the wellbore, and collapsed.

(The person collapsed, not the packer.) A comma is needed after an introductory phrase or clause if its absence would confuse the reader.

Incorrect: If data are inaccurate or incomplete results will not be representative.

Correct: If data are inaccurate or incomplete, results will not be representative.

A comma is usually used to set off an introductory participial phrase. (See guidance on participial phrases.)

Correct: Working quickly as the floodwaters rose, the crew protected the wellsite with sandbags and a pump.

Unless they are restrictive, words that identify a noun or pronoun (appositives) are set off by commas if they follow the noun or pronoun.

Restrictive words or phrases are necessary for the sentence to make sense; nonrestrictive components can be removed from a sentence without changing the meaning. (See guidance on that and which clauses.)

Restrictive: The computer in my office needs a new modem. (There is more than one computer; only the computer in my office needs a modem.)

Nonrestrictive: The computer, which I got last week, needs a new modem. (There is only one computer.)

Words that identify a noun or pronoun are not set off by commas if they precede the noun or pronoun.

Correct: George Wilson, field engineer, began his career with SLB in 1954.

Correct: Field Engineer George Wilson began his career with SLB in 1954.

Correct: Single-phase, or monophasic, flow plots as a straight line.

Place a comma between two adjectives that modify the same noun if the word and could replace the comma without altering the meaning.

Incorrect: The array is used to detect hydrocarbons in thin highly porous formations.

Correct: The array is used to detect hydrocarbons in thin, highly porous formations.

Use commas to set off phrases that give descriptive information that is not essential to the meaning of the sentence.

Do not use commas to set off phrases that are essential to the meaning of the sentence.

Correct: The governing equation for the flow in the skin cells, which includes the inertial resistance effects, is developed next.

Incorrect: All samples, which came from unfractured sections, were obtained with 22-g charges.

Correct: All samples that came from unfractured sections were obtained with 22-g charges.

Use a comma before the conjunction in the last element in a series of three or more elements.

UK English: Although use of this comma is often debated for US English, it is used so consistently in UK English that it is referred to as ‘the Oxford comma’.

Press releases and articles for trade journals follow AP style and use the serial comma only as necessary to avoid ambiguity.

Use a semicolon to punctuate elements in a series of three or more that has commas within the elements.

Incorrect: The fluid modulus for water depends on pressure, temperature and salinity.

Correct: The fluid modulus for water depends on pressure, temperature, and salinity.

Incorrect: The parameters that dictate horizontal well success include permeability anisotropy, formation porosity, saturation, and pressure along the horizontal length, and location of the wellbore in relation to the formation, gas/oil contact, and oil/water contact.

Correct: The parameters that dictate horizontal well success include permeability anisotropy; formation porosity, saturation, and pressure along the horizontal length; and location of the wellbore in relation to the formation, gas/oil contact, and oil/water contact.

A serial comma is not used where an ampersand (&) is used instead of the word and. (See guidance on use of the ampersand.)

Continental European English: In some parts of continental Europe, a comma is used in place of a period to indicate the decimal point.

Correct for continental Europe: EUR 155,25 The Latin abbreviations i.e. and e.g. must always be followed by a comma. If i.e. or e.g. connects two independent clauses, precede it with a semicolon.

If i.e. or e.g. introduces an incomplete clause, enclose the clause within parentheses.

Clauses beginning with i.e. or e.g. can also be set off with an em dash.

The Latin terms i.e. and e.g. may be replaced by their English equivalents, that is and for example, respectively. (See i.e. vs. e.g.)

UK English: A comma may be used but is not required after that is, for example, and namely. To avoid double punctuation, no comma follows the abbreviations i.e. and e.g.

Incorrect: This validation takes place in inverse mode, i.e., a synthetic test data set is generated with the rigorous model.

Correct: This validation takes place in inverse mode; i.e., a synthetic test data set is generated with the rigorous model.

Incorrect: The interpretation can be attempted only if reliable starting conditions, e.g., a good stabilization, can be found for each transient.

Correct for US: The interpretation can be attempted only if reliable starting conditions (e.g., a good stabilization) can be found for each transient.

Correct for US: The interpretation can be attempted only if reliable starting conditions— e.g., a good stabilization—can be found for each transient.

Correct for UK: The interpretation can be attempted only if reliable starting conditions— e.g. a good stabilization—can be found for each transient.

Correct for UK: Several trucks, namely the 15 ton and crew cab pickup, are due for maintenance.

Use commas to separate the parts of addresses and names of geographical places or political divisions.

In an address formatted as a postal address, do not use commas at the ends of lines.

UK English: In text, in is employed to separate city and county instead of a comma.

Incorrect: Data from the Jene field in South Sumatra, Indonesia are used to illustrate the concepts explained in this paper.

Correct: Data from the Jene field in South Sumatra, Indonesia, are used to illustrate the concepts explained in this paper.

Correct for UK: The drilling data from Croydon in Surrey were used for testing the database.

Place commas between the day of the week, the month and day, and the year.

Place a comma after the year preceded by the month and day if the year is not followed by the terminal punctuation of the sentence.

Do not use a comma between the month and the year or after the year if no date is given.

Do not use a comma if the date precedes the month and year, such as in the day-month-year system commonly used in English-speaking countries other than the United States.

Incorrect: This well was drilled on Friday March 14 1986. Correct: This well was drilled on Friday, March 14, 1986.

Correct: This well was drilled on March 14, 1986, in the Austin chalk.

Correct: This well was drilled in March 1986 in the Austin chalk.

Correct: Friday, 14 March 1986 Always place commas inside footnote reference numbers.

Always place commas outside superscript trademark symbols or subscripts that are part of a term.

Incorrect: The nonlinear rise in the inertial resistance has long been recognized13, but its significance was greatly underestimated.

Correct: The nonlinear rise in the inertial resistance has long been recognized,13 but its significance was greatly underestimated.

Incorrect: The maximum withdrawal volume is 1,000 cm,3 but the capacity of the transport container is only 600 cm3.

Correct: The maximum withdrawal volume is 1,000 cm3, but the capacity of the transport container is only 600 cm3.

Always place commas inside single or double quotation marks. UK English: If the comma is not part of the term set off by quotation marks, place it outside the closing quotation marks.

Incorrect: The years between 1865 and 1985 have been called “the century of exploration”, but what should we call the years since then?

Correct for US: The years between 1865 and 1985 have been called “the century of exploration,” but what should we call the years since then?

Correct for UK: The years between 1865 and 1985 have been called ‘the century of exploration’, but what should we call the years since then?

Semicolon Use a semicolon in compound sentences that are not joined by a conjunction.

Incorrect: Sample chambers are available in three sizes, the number of samples recovered per trip is limited by tool weight.

Correct: Sample chambers are available in three sizes; the number of samples recovered per trip is limited by tool weight.

UK English: Use a semicolon to impart greater emphasis to the second clause in a sentence in which the two clauses are linked by a conjunction.

Correct for UK: Integrity is essential; and woe be unto the company without it in today’s business climate.

Use a semicolon to punctuate elements in a series that has commas within the elements.

Incorrect: The parameters that dictate horizontal well success include permeability anisotropy, formation porosity, saturation, and pressure along the horizontal length, and location of the wellbore in relation to the formation, gas/oil contact, and oil/water contact.

Correct: The parameters that dictate horizontal well success include permeability anisotropy; formation porosity, saturation, and pressure along the horizontal length; and location of the wellbore in relation to the formation, gas/oil contact, and oil/water contact.

If a conjunctive adverb is used between clauses of a compound sentence, it is preceded with a semicolon and usually followed by a comma. Conjunctive adverbs are punctuated with commas if they are in the middle or at the end of a clause.

(Conjunctive adverbs are adverbs that are used like conjunctions to introduce clauses— for example, besides, consequently, finally, first, furthermore, hence, however, indeed, likewise, moreover, next, nevertheless, then, therefore, thus. Some adverb phrases also have this function—for example, for example, for instance, on the contrary, on the other hand.)

Incorrect: The epithermal ratio was sensitive to formation porosity, however attempts to characterize the response from field data were subject to the conditions under which the data were acquired.

Correct: The epithermal ratio was sensitive to formation porosity; however, attempts to characterize the response from field data were subject to the conditions under which the data were acquired.

Correct: The blowout was spectacular; it was, however, short-lived.

Always place semicolons outside single or double quotation marks.

Incorrect: The years between 1865 and 1985 may be called “the century of exploration;” since then, improved recovery has been the primary means of increasing reserves.

Correct: The years between 1865 and 1985 may be called “the century of exploration”; since then, improved recovery has been the primary means of increasing reserves.

Colon Use a colon to introduce a series or list unless the introductory clause ends in a verb or preposition.

Incorrect: The trends that occurred were: wellbore storage, partial penetration, and radial flow.

Correct: The trends that occurred were wellbore storage, partial penetration, and radial flow.

Correct: Three trends were observed in the data: wellbore storage, partial penetration, and radial flow.

Capitalize the first word after a colon if it begins a complete sentence or independent clause.

UK English: Use a colon to direct the reader forward: from cause to effect, from general statement to example, etc. It has the same function as namely, that is, because, and therefore. The first word following the colon is not capitalized.

Correct: Examination of the map made us question our plans: Would standard logging trucks be able to negotiate the steep path to the wellsite?

Correct for UK: The pressure increased: the driller increased the mud weight.

Always place colons outside single or double quotation marks.

Incorrect: Each person is ultimately responsible for protecting the “human machinery:” eyes, ears, fingers, and brain.

Correct: Each person is ultimately responsible for protecting the “human machinery”: eyes, ears, fingers, and brain.

UK English: Use a colon to introduce quoted or paraphrased material more formally or emphatically than a comma would.

Correct for UK: Sir Galahad said: ‘Without the PowerDrive™ system we would have drilled all year.’ Dashes Use an em dash, or “long” dash (about as wide as a capital M in a typeface), without spaces before or after to set off parenthetical material. Never precede or follow a dash with a comma. Never begin a line of text with a dash.

Correct: The response is characterized for the three basic lithologies—limestone, sandstone, and dolomite—over a wide range of borehole sizes, mud weights, and fluid salinities.

Use an em dash to introduce a clause that gives emphasis or explanation.

Correct: The author describes coal as a solid colloid—a solid, highly porous dispersion of discrete particles.

Use an en dash, or “short” dash (about as wide as a capital N in a typeface), without spaces before or after to indicate a range such as continuing or inclusive dates, times, or reference numbers.

Do not use an en dash with the word from or between. Correct: 1990–1991 or 1990–91 Incorrect: 1998–02 Correct: 1998–2002 Correct: September–December 1991 Correct: 10 a.m.–3 p.m.

Correct: pages 72–78 Incorrect: We met from 10 a.m.–3 p.m. Correct: We met from 10 a.m. to 3 p.m.

Use the word to when expressing ranges other than dates and times. An en dash may be used for ranges if there are space limitations (for example, tabular material).

(See guidance on the use of to and from.) Incorrect: The skin factor varied from 1– –2.

Correct: The skin factor varied from 1 to –2. Incorrect: The producing interval was at 1,180–2,000 ft.

Correct: The producing interval was at 1,180 to 2,000 ft. Use multiple hyphens in a compound adjective when the terms have internal hyphens or are open compounds.

UK English: An en dash (also called an en rule) is used to express to or and between words of equal importance and to join names Correct: by-product-hydrocarbon mixture Incorrect: 4–20-mA loop-powered display Correct: 4- to 20-mA-loop-powered display Incorrect: New York-Paris flight Correct: New York–Paris flight Correct: Dover–Calais crossing

Apostrophe and quotation marks of joint authors or creators to distinguish them from a single hyphenated name.

Correct for UK: Lloyd-Jones theory (one person) Correct for UK: Lloyd–Jones theory (two people) Correct for UK: A cost–benefit analysis was conducted for installation of the new on–off switch.

An en dash is used for a minus or negative sign; for that reason, avoid ambiguous constructions.

Incorrect: The temperature range was –15––5 degF. Correct: The temperature range was –15 to –5 degF.

Incorrect: 6 - 3 = 3 Correct: 6 – 3 = 3 Hyphens are used to avoid ambiguity in unit modifiers comprising multiple words that precede the modified noun or pronoun. A hyphen is not necessary if each word in the unit modifier individually modifies the noun, if the first word is an adverb ending in –ly, or if the term is well known.

A hyphen is not used in currency terms. Correct: oil-bearing formation Correct: well log analysis Correct: higher-producing zone (a zone that has higher production than others, whereas a “higher producing zone” is physically higher than the others) Correct: An additional USD 5 million was added to the USD 10 million commitment.

Use a hyphen in each of a series of compound adjectives or numeric values and units that modify the same noun and precede it. Multiple hyphens are sometimes necessary to avoid ambiguity.

Correct: Oil- and water-base muds were used in both small- and large-scale tests in single- and multiwell fields.

Incorrect: 2,000–3,000–4,000-psi pressure Correct: 2,000-, 3,000-, and 4,000-psi pressure Correct: 2,000-psi, 3,000-psi, and 4,000-psi pressure Incorrect: 30-m [100-ft]/h rate Correct: 30-m/h [100-ft/h] rate Correct: 3-year-old equipment A joined prefix uses a hyphen if the prefix is placed before a capitalized word, numeral, or hyphenated compound term. A hyphen is also used to separate letter or syllable combinations that may confuse readers or to separate repeated terms in a double prefix.

Correct: The sub-Saharan drilling campaign in the pre-1950s was non-unique except for the use of mini-DSTs.

An apostrophe is used to form a possessive of most singular nouns by adding it and the letter s. Exceptions are certain indefinite pronouns that do not have a possessive form (for example, any, few, none). Only an apostrophe is used to form the possessive of plural nouns if the added s would produce an awkward double sibilant sound, or the term can be rewritten to a phrase using of.

Correct: Texas’s production was greater than Kansas’s. Correct: France and Italy’s border Correct: France’s and Italy’s transportation systems Correct: the surfactants’ properties or the properties of the surfactants Only an apostrophe is used to form the possessive of singular proper nouns that look like plural nouns because they end in the letter s.

Correct: the United States’ participation Correct: Beverly Hills’ history An apostrophe is not used when a plural term is formed by adding an s unless it is necessary to avoid confusion.

For lowercase letters used as letters, form the plural with an apostrophe and a lowercase letter s.

Incorrect: Three BHA’s were shipped. Correct: Three BHAs were shipped.

Correct: x’s and y’s Correct: All the PhDs in the class earned straight As.

Correct: Production steadily declined through the 1950s. Correct: How many “not applicable’s” were entered on the form?

An apostrophe is placed where letters are omitted in contractions. Contractions (typically one word is a verb and the other is a subject or the word not) should be avoided in formal writing.

Incorrect: Periodic huff ’n’ puff operations weren’t successful.

Correct: Periodic huff ’n’ puff operations were not successful.

Quotation marks can be used to set off a key term on its first mention. Do not use both quotation marks and italics for this purpose.

Correct: The pulleys on the crown block of the rig are called sheaves (pronounced “shivs”).

Incorrect: The shale shaker does not remove the “fines” from the mud. Desilters and desanders are used to remove these very small particles because “fines” can erode drilling equipment or make the mud too heavy.

Correct: The shale shaker does not remove the “fines” from the mud. Desilters and desanders are used to remove these very small particles because fines can erode drilling equipment or make the mud too heavy.

Use single quotation marks only inside double quotation marks.

UK English: The use of single and double quotation marks (also called inverted commas) is the opposite of that in US English. Single quotation marks are used to enclose quoted material. Double quotation marks are used to emphasize words or to enclose a quotation within a quotation.

Incorrect: The axial receivers are grouped in two pairs: the ‘far’ and the ‘near.’ Correct for US: The axial receivers are grouped in two pairs: the “far” and the “near.” Correct for US: The instructor said, “The axial receivers are grouped in two pairs: the ‘far’ and the ‘near.’” Correct for UK: The instructor said, ‘The axial receivers are grouped in two pairs: the “far” and the “near”.’

Always place periods and commas inside single or double quotation marks.

Always place semicolons and colons outside single or double quotation marks.

UK English: Only those punctuation points that appeared in the original material should be included within the quotation marks; all others follow the closing quotation mark.

Correct: The years between 1865 and 1985 may be called “the century of exploration.” Correct for US: The years between 1865 and 1985 may be called “the century of exploration”; since then, improved recovery has been the primary means of increasing reserves.

Correct for UK: The years between 1865 and 1985 may be called ‘the century of exploration’.

Correct for UK: The name of the SLB communities of practice came from Archimedes’s cry of discovery: ‘Eureka!’ Other punctuation marks Use a solidus (slash) to express a ratio of word terms or a boundary relation.

Incorrect: length:width ratio Correct: length/width ratio Correct: oil/water contact Ellipsis points are used to indicate omissions within and between sentences, typically in quotations to remove unnecessary material.

Ellipsis points can also be used to suggest faltering or fragmented speech.

If the omission is in the middle of a sentence, three dots are used and are separated from the text and each other by spaces.

If the omission is between sentences and the remaining text is grammatically complete, four dots are used—the first is the period for terminal punctuation.

Incorrect: Pressure profiles…are crucial for optimizing recovery.

Correct: Pressure profiles . . . are crucial for optimizing recovery.

Incorrect: In virgin reservoirs, vertical pressure profiles are obtained to . . . determine completion strategy . . .

Correct: In virgin reservoirs, vertical pressure profiles are obtained to . . . determine completion strategy. . . .

Capitalization Capital letters are used to call attention to certain words (proper nouns and the first word of a sentence) and can help eliminate ambiguity in the meaning of words (for example, china and China). Down style, or the conservative use of capital letters, is preferred for all SLB documents and publications. The following rules deal with specific examples, but when in doubt about whether to capitalize a particular word, consult the list of “Spellings and Terminology.” If a word is not listed there, consult Merriam-Webster’s Collegiate Dictionary (11th ed.) or another resource (see “Suggested reading”).

As addressed in the following two sections, capitalization conventions differ for titles and text headings, body text, and figure titles and labels. Capitalization guidance for tabular material is in “Table examples.” This capitalization guidance is used in conjunction with template styles, such as in the “Brand Elements and Templates” and “Writing for Our Public Websites.”

Words and text Capitalize only the first word in a complete sentence and proper nouns. Avoid using initial capital letters to imply uniqueness, importance, or authority for nouns that are not proper nouns or for generic mentions of proper nouns.

Do not capitalize a parenthetical sentence within another sentence.

A term or proper name that begins with a lowercase letter is capitalized at the beginning of a sentence unless it is a company name or a mark of SLB that begins with a lowercase letter. In references, follow the author’s preference for capitalization. A sentence can also be rewritten to avoid having an initial lowercase letter.

The term pH is always lowercased. Incorrect: The tool was assembled with a Centralizer and additional Sub.

Correct: The tool was assembled with a centralizer and additional sub.

Incorrect: Personnel were assigned to groups on the basis of their training (The length of their employment with the company was not a factor).

Correct: Personnel were assigned to groups on the basis of their training (the length of their employment with the company was not a factor).

Incorrect: de Luna’s seminal research led to the development of the innovative eFire™ electronic firing head system.

Correct: De Luna’s seminal research led to the development of the innovative eFire™ electronic firing head system.

Correct: eFire™ electronic firing head systems use field-proven technology.

Incorrect: PH is not a reliable indicator. Correct: pH is not a reliable indicator.

Correct: The pH is not a reliable indicator. Correct: Do not rely on pH as an indicator.

Capitalize the first word after a colon if it begins a complete sentence or independent clause.

UK English: The first word following the colon is not capitalized.

Correct: Examination of the map made us question our plans: Would standard logging trucks be able to negotiate the steep path to the wellsite?

Correct for UK: The pressure increased: the driller increased the mud weight.

Capitalize titles of rank or position that precede a name. Do not capitalize titles of rank or position that follow a name. Titles used without the name or generically are usually lowercased.

Title capitalization exceptions include displayed lists of names and titles and certain promotional materials.

Incorrect: Division manager George Wilson was awarded . . . Correct: Division Manager George Wilson was awarded . . .

Incorrect: George Wilson, Division Manager, was awarded . . .

Correct: George Wilson, division manager, was awarded . . . Incorrect: George Wilson was named Division Manager.

Correct: George Wilson was named division manager. Capitalize names of companies, institutions, and organizations. Capitalize Geounit and Technology (external) or Business Line (internal) for SLB entities. Capitalize geographic or generic terms if they are part of Correct: Gulf Coast Division Incorrect: the Division Correct: the division Correct: The EUR Geounit is headquartered in Aberdeen.

place names. Capitalize descriptive terms that denote a defined region. But do not capitalize generic terms when used alone or descriptively.

Correct: Trinity River Correct: Trinity and Sabine Rivers Correct: US Continental Shelf (subsequent mentions can be just “Shelf”) Correct: Mounts McKinley and Olympus Correct: West Coast Correct: western Harris County Correct: the valley of the Mississippi River Capitalize the names of academic degrees used in complete form. Do not capitalize academic degrees when referred to in general terms.

Correct: Bachelor of Science degree Correct: bachelor’s degree Correct: doctor of philosophy Capitalize nouns and adjectives designating parts of the world or regions. Do not capitalize adjectives that refer to a direction or general location.

Correct: Middle East Correct: South Pacific Incorrect: The fault is downthrown to the West.

Correct: The fault is downthrown to the west. Geologic place names: Do not capitalize descriptive uses of words such as basin, belt, delta, field, pay, play, pool, reservoir, sand, shale, and trend. They are capitalized as part of a proper name.

Correct: San Juan Basin Correct: A famous East Texas field is Luling Field Correct: These wells in the Eagle Ford Play target the Eagle Ford Shale and Woodbine Formation.

Capitalize only eponyms, not the accompanying names of devices, methods, theories, techniques, systems, or laws.

Correct: Darcy’s law Correct: Reynolds number Capitalize Well, Track, and Booth used with specific alphanumeric identification.

Correct: Well 238 was fractured but Bessie Smith 1, the offset well, was shut in.

Correct: The numerous fractures imaged in Track 3 of the log are in the same interval as the gas crossover in the neutron density track.

Correct: Visit SLB at Booth 435. Do not capitalize the names of chemical elements or chemical compounds.

Incorrect: The pressure drop increased the precipitation of Calcium Carbonate scale.

Correct: The pressure drop increased the precipitation of calcium carbonate scale.

Capitalize the names of formally named geochronologic (time) and chronostratigraphic Correct: Jurassic Period Correct: Early Pliocene Epoch

(time-rock) units. Time scales are available from the International Commission on Stratigraphy and the US Geological Survey.

The preceding modifiers, such as early, describe the position of the unit and must be used carefully. Early, middle, and late are restricted to time contexts and are used with the rank units eon, era, period, epoch, and age (listed in order of decreasing magnitude).

The corresponding modifiers lower, middle, and upper are restricted to the rocks themselves and their position and are used with the rank units eonothem, erathem, system, series, and stage.

Incorrect: The sediments were deposited in the Lower Jurassic.

Incorrect: The sediments were deposited in the early Jurassic.

Correct: The sediments were deposited in the Early Jurassic. Correct: Early Jurassic highstands resulted in the deposition of sediments.

Capitalize earth when it is used as the proper name of the planet.

Correct: Gravitational effects between the moon and the Earth influenced measurement of the wellbore fluids.

Correct: earth-moving equipment Correct: His attitude was down to earth.

Never use all capital or initial capital letters for emphasis in text.

Incorrect: The Electric Log was a Revolutionary development. Correct: The electric log was a revolutionary development.

Titles, headings, and figures Templates for print materials are described in the SLB “Brand Elements and Templates.” Guidance for the slb.com framework and page components is in “Writing for Our Public Websites.” The capitalization guidance in this section is used in conjunction with the styles specified for titles, headings, and figures.

When using initial-capitalization style (also called headline-style capitalization), capitalize the first word, nouns, pronouns, verbs, adverbs, adjectives, and all other words of five or more letters. Exceptions are company names or marks of SLB or other companies and unit symbols that begin with a lowercase letter.

Incorrect: An Innovative Approach To Correct Density Measurements In Clays Correct: An Innovative Approach to Correct Density Measurements in Clays Incorrect: EBay is Selling EFire Systems Correct: eBay Is Selling eFire Systems Incorrect: EFire Systems Available on EBay Correct: eFire Systems Available on eBay Incorrect: Increased Capacity 500 Bbl/d Correct: Increased Capacity 500 bbl/d Capitalize the first word after a colon in a title or heading.

Correct: Calculating Density from Clays: A Maximum Porosity Correct: Calculating density from clays: A maximum porosity Capitalize both parts of a compound adjective in a title using initial-capitalization style.

Correct: Ratio-Based Epithermal Neutron Porosity for Air-Filled Boreholes

For figure titles, capitalize the first word, nouns, pronouns, adverbs, and all other words of five or more letters.

For figure callouts, capitalize only the first word and proper nouns. If the first element of a callout is a numeral, the first following word is not capitalized (for example, 15-ft interval).

Exceptions are company names or marks of SLB that begin with a lowercase letter.

Figure 3. The QUANTUM™ PERFPAC™ sand control system provides one-trip perforating and gravel packing.

For axis labels on graphs, capitalize only the first word and proper nouns. Unit values follow, separated from the label by a comma.

Comparison of horizontal permeability measured by mini-DSTs, cores, and IPTTs shows generally good agreement of the different measurements.

Abbreviations, acronyms, and symbols Abbreviations and acronyms are important space savers in technical writing and help provide the maximum amount of information in limited space. Consider the audience’s level of knowledge of the subject when deciding whether to use abbreviations. Then, use abbreviations only if you are sure the reader will understand them.

Oilfield, technical, and nontechnical abbreviations are in the list of “Spellings and Terminology.” If an abbreviation is not listed there, consult Merriam-Webster’s Collegiate Dictionary (11th ed.).

The term symbol is used for the abbreviations of units of measure that are used with numeric values. SLB uses the American Petroleum Institute–Le Système International d’Unités (API-SI) unit symbols specified in Recommended Practice (RP) 66, which is under the stewardship of the Petrotechnical Open Software Corporation (POSC). Only units of the SI and the API-SI supplementary unit symbols and derived unit symbols are used to express the values of quantities.

Exceptions and their usage are explicitly identified in this guidance and in the set of SLB “Unit Symbols and Prefixes.” Additional exceptions for the use of nonpreferred unit symbols are as required by contract, law, or regulation or as appropriate for the material. “Guidelines for Unit Symbols” further details the use of unit symbols and unit names.

General format and usage Consult the list of “Spellings and Terminology” for a list of abbreviations that can be used as stand-alone terms. Otherwise, spell out an abbreviated term the first time it is used with the abbreviation enclosed in parentheses following the term. Thereafter, the abbreviation may be used alone.

In tables, titles, and mark identifiers, an abbreviation that is not stand-alone may be used without spelled-out introduction in consideration of space constraints. These abbreviations must be defined in accompanying text or a table footnote.

Incorrect: The United States (US) rig count rose 2% over the previous quarter.

Correct: The US rig count rose 2% over the previous quarter. Incorrect: Today, efficient drilling includes PDC bits, oil-base mud, and measurements while drilling.

Correct: Today, efficient drilling includes polycrystalline diamond compact (PDC) bits, oil-base mud, and measurements while drilling.

In recent years, the use of PDC bits has boosted the average penetration rate to more than 100 m/d.

Use a spelled-out term (for example, and instead of & [ampersand] and at instead of @ [“at” symbol]) instead of a symbol for the word unless the symbol is a component of a company name, abbreviation, or e-mail address. See Section 5.1.1 in the “Writing for Our Public Websites” guidelines of the Communications Handbook on use of ampersand in writing for the Web.

Do not use the number symbol (#), also called the hash, hashtag, or pound sign, as a garnishment but keep it in official names.

An exemption is using # in “#1” for superlative, informal statements.

Correct: Audits of D&A wells are conducted by Gain & Loss Inc.

Correct: E-mail drilling@slb.com for information. Incorrect: Valves #1 and #3 failed.

Correct: Valves 1 and 3 failed. Correct: The MC 941 #3 well is in approximately 4,000 ft of water.

Incorrect: The no. 1 name in rock-ready bits Incorrect: The number-one name in rock- ready bits Correct: The #1 name in rock-ready bits Incorrect: Casing Collar Locator (CCL) Correct: casing collar locator (CCL)

Do not use initial capital letters for the spelled- out form of an abbreviation unless the words are proper nouns.

Correct: Petrotechnical Open Software Corporation (POSC) An abbreviation retains its capitalization in figure callouts and table headings and entries.

Most acronyms are pronounced as words; most initialisms are pronounced as a series of letters. Use a or an as the indefinite article depending on the first sound of the abbreviation.

Correct: an FAQ Correct: a NACE standard Minor variations to what an abbreviation stands for can be read in context and do not require reintroduction. However, plurals are formed from the abbreviation itself (for example, the plural of BHA for bottomhole assembly is BHAs).

Correct: The cost to plug and abandon (P&A) the well was included in the contract. However, test results were so promising that the well was completed instead of P&A [read as “plugged and abandoned”].

The plural forms of abbreviations for singular terms are formed by adding an s to the abbreviation. An apostrophe is not used for a plural form of an abbreviation unless it is necessary to avoid confusion.

Incorrect: A new mobile offshore drilling unit (MODU) was designed for the unique drilling conditions, and 15 MODU’s were quickly shipped to the field.

Correct: A new mobile offshore drilling unit (MODU) was designed for the unique drilling conditions, and 15 MODUs were quickly shipped to the field.

Close up initials in a person’s name. Incorrect: P. D. Smith Correct: P.D. Smith Correct: W.N.T. Doran Abbreviate academic degrees without periods or spaces.

Correct: BS, BSc, BFA Correct: MBA, PhD, DSc The names of months can be spelled out or abbreviated; use one style consistently in text.

Abbreviations are useful in graphics and tables because of space constraints. See guidance on the use of months in references.

Correct: Aug. 1988 or Aug 1988 or August Correct: Aug. 24, 1988 or 24 Aug. 1988 Correct: Aug 24, 1988 or 24 Aug 1988 Symbols for chemical elements may be used without introducing them following the spelled- out element name. Chemical symbols have an initial capital letter. Do not mix symbols with words in the name of chemical compounds.

Correct: The fluid was saturated with Fe and Mn. Correct: The fluid was saturated with iron (Fe) and manganese (Mn).

Incorrect: The fluid was rich in Na chloride. Correct: The fluid was rich in sodium chloride.

Correct: The fluid was rich in NaCl.

Time zones are stand-alone abbreviations and given in parentheses immediately following the time when the time is used.

For promotion of trade shows or events (e.g., OTC, ADIPEC, etc.), it is recommended to use the time zone promoted by the trade show or event for consistency.

GMT—Greenwich mean time EST—eastern standard time EDT—eastern daylight time CST—central standard time CDT—central daylight time CT—central time MST—mountain standard time MDT—mountain daylight time PST—Pacific standard time PDT—Pacific daylight time UTC—coordinated universal time Incorrect: The event starts at 4:45 p.m. CST.

Correct: The event starts at 4:45 p.m. (CST). Correct: The event starts at 13:00

## (UTC–05:00).

Correct: All times are UTC+04:00. Correct: All times are Houston local.

Common oilfield chemical compounds may be referred to by their formulas (element symbols) without first introducing them following the spelled-out name:

CaCO3 (calcium carbonate) CH4 (methane) CO2 (carbon dioxide) HCl (hydrochloric acid) HF (hydrofluoric acid) H2O (water) H2S (hydrogen sulfide) KCl (potassium chloride) N2 (nitrogen molecule or molecular nitrogen) NaCl (sodium chloride) H2 (hydrogen molecule) O2 (oxygen molecule or molecular oxygen) SiO2 (silica) CaBr2 (calcium bromide) NaBr2 (sodium bromide) ZnBr2 (zinc bromide) Ca(OH)2 (calcium hydroxide, lime) Na(OH)2 (sodium hydroxide, caustic soda) BaSO4 (barite).

Do not mix symbols with words in the name of chemical compounds.

Correct: The well was treated with first HCl and then HF. Correct: The well was treated with first hydrochloric acid (HCl) and then hydrofluoric acid (HF).

Hydrocarbon compounds are expressed by the number of carbon atoms. methane C1 ethane C2 propane C3 butane C4 pentane C5 hexane and higher C6+

Words such as figure, equation, and volume may be abbreviated when followed by a number except when used at the beginning of a sentence. Do not abbreviate table, chapter, or appendix. These words and their allowed abbreviations are capitalized if they precede a numeral or letter used for specific identification.

Incorrect: This is obvious in Figure 8. Correct: This is obvious in Fig. 8.

Correct: Figure 8 shows the obvious relation. Correct: Eq. 4 Correct: Vol. 39 Correct: Table 1-7 Correct: Chapter 4 Correct: Appendix C Unit symbols of measure When accompanied by a numeric value, units of measure are formatted as symbols, not the unit names. The SLB “Unit Symbols and Prefixes” list is based on API-SI RP 66 units.

Do not mix unit names with unit symbols. The exceptions to the use of symbols include day, hour, minute, second, and year, which are spelled out for nontechnical uses. Week and month are not abbreviated.

Do not use the pound sign (#). Powers in unit symbols can be superscript trademark symbol or baseline; one format should be used consistently in a document.

The unit symbol is separated by a space from the number or joined with hyphen if the number-symbol pair is used as a compound adjective preceding the modified noun. (See guidance on compound words.) Exceptions are the degree symbol used for angles and the percent symbol or as required by contract, law, or regulation.

Incorrect: 10 meters Correct: 10 m Incorrect: We met from 8:30 a.m. to 10 p.m. every Thursday for 5 yr.

Correct: We met from 8:30 a.m. to 10 p.m. every Thursday for 5 years.

Incorrect: 3 kilogram/m, 3 kg/meter, or 3 kg per m Correct: 3 kg/m Incorrect: 250 # Incorrect: 250 pounds Incorrect: 250 lb Correct: 250 lbm Incorrect: The 8, 11, and 14-in wellbores developed only minimal scale.

Correct: The 8-, 11-, and 14-in wellbores developed only minimal scale.

Correct: The 8-in, 11-in, and 14-in wellbores developed only minimal scale.

Incorrect: The average density was 5 g/cm3, and the maximum was 10 g/cm3.

Correct: The average density was 5 g/cm3, and the maximum was 10 g/cm3.

Correct: The average density was 5 g/cm3, and the maximum was 10 g/cm3.

For a range or two or more serial values with the same unit, the unit symbol can be listed with each value or with the final value; whichever format is used must be consistently applied in a document. It must be clear which symbol belongs to a number, and a number and its accompanying symbol may not be typographically separated. Repeat nonalphabetic symbols with each numeric value in a range or series.

Correct: alternating increments of 5 cm and 10 cm Correct: alternating increments of 5 and 10 cm Correct: For a saturation range of 7% to 19%, angle variance was from 10° to 25°.

Incorrect: 35 × 48 cm Correct: 35 cm × 48 cm Incorrect: 70 g ± 2 Correct: 70 g ± 2 g Incorrect: The value is expressed in km.

Correct: The value is expressed in kilometers.

Do not use a symbol for a unit of measure that is not accompanied by a number unless the unit is complex.

Incorrect: The thermal conductivity was recorded in watts per meter kelvin.

Correct: The thermal conductivity was recorded in W/(m.K). Unit symbols do not have ending punctuation except at the end of a sentence. Use numerals, not letters, for square and cube (an exception is psi for pounds per square inch). Do not add an s to a unit symbol to indicate plural.

Incorrect: 5 inches Incorrect: 5 in. Correct: 5 in Incorrect: The interval drilled was 5 m Correct: The interval drilled was 5 m.

Incorrect: A 5-m. interval was drilled. Correct: A 5-m interval was drilled.

Incorrect: square feet or sq ft Correct: ft2 or ft2 Incorrect: 10 cms Correct: 10 cm SI units are preferred to US customary oilfield units that are accepted for use with RP 66 (for example, m3 or m3 for cubic meter is preferred to bbl for barrel). Exceptions are nominal diameters of pipe (tubing and casing), bit sizes, and nominal tool diameters, for which the preferred unit is the inch (See guidance on specific usage conventions.)

Equivalent values are given in secondary units in square brackets following the primary units only if necessary for the intended audience (for example, as historical secondary units). The structure of multiple sets of units should be parallel.

Correct: [20 cm] 8 in Incorrect: 61 m [20-ft] trajectory Correct: 61-m [20-ft] trajectory Incorrect: The 1920 calculation of reserves was 5 Bcf.

Correct: The 1920 calculation of reserves was 140 million m3 [5 Bcf].

Incorrect: The wellbore was reamed with an 11.43-cm [4 1/2-in] bit to 4,500 m [14,760 ft] before the 7.62-cm [3-in] casing was run.

Correct: The wellbore was reamed with a 4 1/2-in bit to 4,500 m [14,760 ft] before the 3-in casing was run.

Do not mix other information with unit symbols. Incorrect: 20-mL H2O/kg or 20 mL of water/kg Correct: water content is 20 mL/kg For scientific or statistical writing, express percentages with the % symbol. In other text spell out the word percent.

Incorrect: The outcrop cores were vacuum saturated with 2 percent NaCl solution.

Correct: The outcrop cores were vacuum saturated with 2% NaCl solution.

Correct: The crew was urged to give 100 percent on the job. Numbers Spelled-out and numeral forms

Spell out the numbers one through nine except when used with units of measure or in expressions with a mathematical sense. (Do not repeat a spelled-out number in numerals, which is a legal convention.) Spell out ordinal numbers from first through ninth; use integers for 10th and higher. Use only numerals for statistical and tabular data, dates, addresses, and time. Very large or very small numbers in a technical context can be expressed in scientific notation. Large, rounded numbers can be spelled out or expressed as a combination of numerals and words, but this format is not used for precise values with units of measure.

Incorrect: five (5) wells Correct: five wells Correct: 10 wells Correct: 5 ft Correct: Rinsing the sample two times reduced the likelihood of contamination by a factor of 4.

Correct: second Correct: 22nd Correct: 3 p.m. Incorrect: August 12th, 2000 Correct: August 12, 2000 Correct: The value of Avogadro’s number for the number of atoms or other particles in a gram mole of a substance is 6.023 × 1023.

Correct: almost a thousand years Correct: The country’s population is about 2 million.

If numerals are used for one of the numbers in a specific category or series in a sentence, use numerals for all category mentions in the sentence.

Correct: Together the three fields have six producing wells and nine injection wells.

Correct: Together the three fields have 6 producing wells and 12 injection wells.

Correct: The reservoir sand was 19 m thick in one well. Spell out a number that begins a sentence or recast the sentence.

Incorrect: 45 days later . . . Correct: Forty-five days later . . .

Correct: After 45 days . . . Spell out the first number to distinguish between two juxtaposed numbers.

Incorrect: 10 2-min tests Correct: ten 2-min tests

There are several options for presenting time of day in numerical format, but one style should be used consistently throughout a document.

Digital clocks have increased the use of four- digit numbers in all countries.

In the US, a colon separates the hour and minutes and a.m. or p.m. is used. Use a colon to separate the hour and minutes in 24-h time.

If writing for a local audience, select the option appropriate to the region.

In the UK, a full stop separates the hour and minutes. When exact times are emphasized, the 24-h system is customary, and a colon separates the hour and minutes.

In Norway, the 24-h system is used. There is no punctuation between hours and minutes, and a.m. and p.m. are not used.

In some parts of Southeast Asia and the Middle East, four digits are used, and a colon or period separates the hour and minutes.

For promotion of trade shows or events (e.g., OTC, ADIPEC, etc.), it is recommended to use the time (i.e., 24-h time system vs. 12-h time system) promoted by the trade show or event for consistency.

Customary for US: Cookies are served in the cafeteria at 9:15 a.m. and 4:30 p.m.

Customary for US: Operations resumed at 08:00 when the weather cleared.

Customary for UK: The cafeteria begins tea service at 4.30 p.m.

Customary for UK: Trains depart for London at 08:12 and 16:33.

Customary for Norway: 0800 (8 a.m.), 2400 (midnight) Other options: 08:30 or 08.30 Numeral formats In the US, which is transitioning to the metric system, numbers with four or more digits are typically separated into groups of three digits by a comma.

US monetary values and US patent numbers are always written with commas.

In countries that use the SI system, numbers with four digits typically are set closed.

Numbers with five or more digits typically are separated into groups of three digits by either set spaces or commas. When set spaces are used before the decimal point (which can also be represented by a comma), spaces are also used, if necessary, after the decimal. Commas are never used after the decimal point.

Dates, pages, and addresses never use commas or spaces. Incorrect for US: 4000 Incorrect for US: 4 000 Correct for US: 4,000 Incorrect: 10000 Correct for US: 10,000 Correct for UK: 10 000 Incorrect for US: 10,000 strokes in 1500 min Correct for US: 10,000 strokes in 1,500 min Form plurals of numerals by adding an s. Incorrect: During the 1990’s Correct: During the 1990s

Use a colon to express a ratio between numerals. Incorrect: The ratio of engineers to drillers at the wellsite was 3/1.

Correct: The ratio of engineers to drillers at the wellsite was 3:1.

If depths require anonymity, use letters to alias the digits. For example, on the depth track of a log if all depths have the same number of digits (for example, all four-digit or all five-digit numbers), replace the thousands digits with an X for 1,000 to 9,999 (X,000 to X,999) or with XX for 10,000 and deeper (XX,000). Do not have X,000 and XX,000 in the same depth track because that reveals that the corresponding depths are obviously 9,000 and 10,000.

If a specific depth or depth interval must be differentiated in an aliased depth track, use different letters in alphabetic sequence. For example:

## XX,000

## XX,500

## XY,000

## XY,500

## XZ,000

## XZ,500

Spell out common fractions when they are used alone in the text. A spelled-out fraction uses an internal hyphen unless it has a hyphenated component.

Use numbers when the fraction is combined with a whole number or when used with a unit of measure.

For web or digital collateral, nonstacked (e.g., 3/8) vs stacked (e.g., ⅜) fractions (are preferred due to limited formatting.

Correct: one-third the cost Correct: The performance rating increased by thirty-two hundredths of a point.

Incorrect: Two and three-fourths in Correct: 5 1/2 days A decimal point must be preceded and followed by a numeral.

The decimal point typically is represented by a period or baseline dot.

Continental Europe: The decimal point typically is represented by a comma.

Incorrect: .35 cm Correct: 0.35 cm Incorrect: 5. Correct: 5.0 Correct: 36,333.33 Correct for continental Europe: 36 333,33 An en dash is used for a minus or negative sign; for that reason, avoid ambiguous constructions.

Incorrect: The temperature range was –15––5 degF. Correct: The temperature range was –15 to –5 degF.

Incorrect: 6 - 3 = 3 Correct: 6 – 3 = 3

Numerals and unit symbols Formats When a unit of measure is accompanied by a numeric value and used as an adjective, a hyphen is placed between the number and the unit.

Incorrect: 5 in casing Correct: 5-in casing Correct: 3-ft zone Incorrect: 30-m [100-ft]/h rate Correct: 30-m/h [100-ft/h] rate The SLB “Unit Symbols and Prefixes” list of RP 66–based unit symbols gives preference to the SI unit symbols over US customary oilfield units. Exceptions are nominal diameters of pipe (tubing and casing), bit sizes, and nominal tool diameters, for which the preferred unit is the inch. (See guidance on specific usage conventions.)

If desired, SI and US customary units may both be given; usually the SI unit symbol is listed first with the secondary unit system given within brackets. The conversion factors for the SLB unit symbols are exact values or to seven- digit accuracy and are primarily from The SI Metric System of Units and SPE Metric Standard (June 1984, 2nd printing).

For guidance on rounding numbers in making unit conversions, see page 57 of Guide for the Use of the International System of Units (SI).

This US National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) publication is based on International Organization for Standardization (ISO) conventions.

Correct: Measurements were taken at 5-m intervals. Correct: The second dome was drilled in 60 m [200 ft] of water.

Correct: For use in high-temperature conditions (i.e., >204 degC [>400 degF]), the tool must be modified.

Incorrect: 30-m [100-ft/h] rate Correct: 30-m/h [100-ft/h] rate Correct: The 5.5-in [139.7-mm] tool became stuck in 7-in casing.

Correct: The 6 5/8-in casing used in the completion had a wall thickness of 0.288 in [7.32 mm].

Specific usage conventions Tubing diameters for sizes 1.050 through 2.063 in are presented in decimal inches to three decimal places. Sizes 2 3/8 in and larger are presented in fractions to designate API nominal size, but measured diameters and wall thickness, etc., are presented in decimal inches to three decimal places.

Casing diameters for API nominal sizes are presented in fractions, but measured diameters and wall thickness, etc., are presented in decimal inches to three decimal places.

Correct: The 1.315-in tubing has a wall thickness of 0.133 in [33.78 mm].

Correct: This 6 5/8-in casing has a wall thickness of 0.288 in.

Correct: 4.5-in 12.75-lbm/ft casing (no comma in industry usage) Bit sizes and drill collars are expressed as nominal sizes in inches and fractions.

Correct: Although the final section was drilled with an 8 1/2-in bit, the wellbore diameter measured by the 4.5-in caliper tool was 12 in.

Tool outside and inside diameters (ODs and IDs, respectively) are expressed in decimal inches to the number of decimal places required to correctly express the fraction. For example, 1 11/16 in is expressed as 1.6875 in, 4 1/2 in is expressed as 4.5 in, 6 3/4 in is expressed as 6.75 in, and 9 5/8 in is expressed as 9.625 in.

Correct: Even the slim 2.125-in tool could not pass the restriction where the borehole had collapsed.

Conversion of tool diameters and measured dimensions of casing and tubing in US customary units is to millimeters only and displayed to two decimal places.

Correct: Corrosion and wear had reduced the thickness of the 4 1/2-in casing to 0.199 in [5.05 mm] from the original 0.205 in [5.21 mm].

API connections are expressed as a fraction and API type, but the unit (inch) is not included and no SI conversion is made.

Incorrect: 6 5/8-in Reg Incorrect: 6 5/8-in [168.27-mm] Reg Correct: 6 5/8 Reg Length dimensions are usually presented in centimeters to one decimal place or in meters to one or two decimal places. Critical lengths such as measure points may be expressed to three decimal places.

In table columns or rows listing like dimensions, use only one convention. For example, if the tool dimensions are all in meters, even short sections should be given in meters (0.43 m, not 43 cm) to avoid the unintentional calculation error of adding centimeters to meters. Similarly, in a table or figure use only one convention for related dimensions that may be part of a calculation (for example, all lengths may be centimeters, but all diameters may be decimal inches).

Incorrect: Removing the 30-cm sub reduced the tool length to 3.1 m.

Correct: Removing the 30-cm sub reduced the tool length to 310 cm.

Tool resolution is expressed in centimeters or meters without decimals.

Correct: The first tool has a vertical depth resolution of 15 cm and a depth of investigation of 25 to 125 cm.

Units of temperature do not use unit symbols with a degree symbol (°). Kelvin temperatures are conventionally written without the degree symbol.

Unsigned temperature values are assumed to be positive, so a plus sign closed up to the temperature is used only as necessary to avoid ambiguity.
