The equivalence of the temperature units is 1 degC = 1.8 degF or 1 degF = 0.56 degC.

Temperature conversions are in the SLB “Unit Symbols and Prefixes” list of RP 66–based unit symbols.

Incorrect: 109°C [228°F] Correct: 109 degC [228 degF] Correct: 114 K Incorrect: The temperature range was –15 to +5 degF.

Correct: The temperature range was –15 to 5 degF. Correct: –10 degC to 150 degC [14 degF to 302 degF]

Nuclide descriptors are arranged around an element symbol as follows: • number of atoms as a right subscript • mass number of an isotope as a left superscript trademark symbol (or hyphenate it to the element name) • atomic number of an atom (used only for nuclear chemistry) as a left subscript • ionic charge as a right superscript trademark symbol (if the charge number is 1, only the sign is shown) • oxidation number (roman numeral) as a right superscript trademark symbol or in line and closed up in parentheses following the element name or symbol.

Correct: H2S Correct: The large C1 peak corresponds to the methane content.

Correct: 137Cs Incorrect: 137cesium Correct: cesium-137 Incorrect: Na1+ Correct: Na+ Incorrect: Ca++, CO3– – Correct: Ca2+, CO32– Correct: MnIII Correct: Mn(III) Correct: manganese(III) Monetary amounts are formatted as numerals preceded by the three-letter currency code from ISO Standard ISO 4217.

The currency code typically consists of a two- letter country code followed by a one-letter currency code.

Very large or rounded amounts can be written as numerals preceding a numeric unit, but do not use the ambiguous terms billion or trillion because they have multiple meanings.

A hyphen is not used in currency terms. Correct: USD 5,000 (US dollar) Correct: USD 10 million Correct: CAD 800 (Canadian dollar) Correct: GBP 14 (UK pound sterling) Correct: EUR 620 (European Union euro) Correct: JPY 80 (Japanese yen) Correct: An additional USD 5 million was added to the USD 10 million commitment.

Mathematics Equations follow the same rules of grammar and punctuation that apply to sentences of text.

Mathematics is written in sentences; terms and operational symbols function as subjects, verbs, conjunctions, or adjectives and are formatted accordingly. Correct grammatical structure must also be maintained in text sentences that contain, precede, or follow equations. Complete sentences preceding equations may end in a period or colon.

Symbols and special characters should be inserted in Microsoft Office documents with ANSI codes because they are more stable than those inserted in a document by using the Insert menu or keyboard shortcuts. To insert a symbol or character code, NUM LOCK must be on. Hold down the ALT key and use the numeric keypad to type in the four-numeral code. Similar character codes are used for Macintosh computers.

In long documents or those that are mathematically intensive, all letter symbols should be defined in a formal, alphabetical nomenclature in the backmatter.

Set all letters used as mathematical terms (either as quantities or numerical values) in italic type except for whole words, contiguous letters that form abbreviations, or letters that correspond to proper nouns. Numerals, Greek alphabet characters, and abbreviations such as log (logarithm), sin (sine), and exp (exponential function) are also set in roman type.

Letter symbols must be single letters; their sub- and superscript trademark symbols can be multiple letters, numerals, or both.

Correct: (59)loga x Correct: vertical permeability = kv or kvertical Correct: maximum temperature = Tmax Correct: Reynolds number = NRe Correct: longitudinal relaxation time = T1 Incorrect: The tool response (TR) is defined as TR = f(V1, V2, . . . , Vn, Vf).

Correct: The tool response (TR) is defined as X = f(V1, V2, . . . , Vn, Vf).

If commas or operational signs are required in elisions, they should come after each term and after the three ellipsis dots when followed by a final term.

Incorrect: a1, a2, . . . an Correct: a1, a2, . . . , an Incorrect: x = 0, 1, 2 . . .

Correct: x = 0, 1, 2, . . . An operational symbol used as an adjective is not spaced apart from the term it modifies; a symbol used as a verb or conjunction has a space before and after it.

Correct: an error of ±3 cm Correct: –x2(a + b) = –2 Correct: Eq. 5 is true if g < 4.

Do not use an asterisk (*) as an operational symbol for multiplication because the asterisk is reserved as a footnote. Multiplication may be indicated by a times sign (×) or multiplier dot (·) (a baseline dot [.] is used within unit symbols), but only one of these operational symbols should be consistently applied in a document.

Incorrect: A = 2B * F2 Correct: A = 2B × F2 Correct: A = 2B · F2 Correct: A = 2B(F2) Correct: 5 ft × 5 ft Correct: 5(20) An en dash is used for a minus or negative sign; for that reason, avoid ambiguous constructions.

Incorrect: The temperature range was –15––5 degF. Correct: The temperature range was –15 to –5 degF.

Incorrect: 6 - 3 = 3 Correct: 6 – 3 = 3 Use a colon to separate parts of a ratio or proportion expressed in numerals.

Correct: a 3:1 mixture

Expressions displayed on separate lines should be centered within the margins. The equation number should be placed flush with the right-hand margin on the last line of the equation. Enclose the number within parentheses.

Equations are generally numbered in sequence throughout the body of the text: (1), (2), (3), etc. Label two-part equations as (1a) and (1b) rather than (1) and (1a). If equations appear in appendices, number them in independent sequence corresponding to each appendix.

Equations in Appendix A are labeled (A-1), (A- 2), etc., and those in Appendix B are (B-1), (B- 2), etc.

If there are chemical reactions displayed in the same document, they may be numbered with a separate sequence of letters or roman numerals.

Correct: Therefore, Eq. 6 may be reduced to abc = def, (7) where a equals 2.

When referring to numbered equations in text, omit the parentheses from the number.

Correct: We substitute Eq. 6 into Eq. 7. When an equation will not fit on one line, try to break it at the equals sign. If this is not possible, break the equation after a complete term and begin the next line with a sign. If the equation requires more than two lines, align each line on operational or descriptive signs.

If possible, do not break fractions or expressions in parentheses, brackets, and braces or after radical signs.

Correct: ct= (0.7)(10–5) + 0.3(3 × 10–6) + (3 × 10–6) = 1.09 × 10–5 psi–1. (2) Use the solidus (/) or format the denominator with a negative exponent for equations that occur in text rather than as display. Terms in display equations in a document should be consistently formatted stacked or in a one-line form (solidus or negative exponent).

Correct: It was assumed that a/b = c/d = 1. Correct: It was assumed that ab–1 = 1.

Incorrect:       +	= z y b a	x Correct:       +	= z y b a x The preferred order for signs of aggregation is parentheses, brackets, and braces, working from the inside of the equation outward: { [ ( { [ ( ) ] } ) ] } Correct: 3{[A(B – C/D) + E]2} = F Lists in text List types

Lists within text can be used to present a series of analogous elements. All items of a list should have the same syntactic structure (i.e., parallel construction).

Long lists can overwhelm readers, who need to be able to comprehend a list as a whole and see how the parts are related. Research on short-term “working” memory indicates that most people can remember between three and nine items for short periods. To improve the readability and usability of lists, the recommended limits for the number of items are • nine items maximum for information presented in print form • seven items maximum for information presented online.

If a list contains more than 10 list items, reorganize the list into multiple separate lists of no more than 10 items each or use sublists to group related items. Long lists of reference information (usually presented in alphabetical order) can be acceptable because readers look up selected information from the list rather than read the list from end to end.

List types are as follows: • ordered (consisting of items that are sequential in hierarchy or action, such as steps to a procedure) • unordered • defining (used for reference or appendix material, such as a glossary or dictionary).

For ordered lists, introduce each item with a numeral. Typically, a list with sequential items is individually numbered (i.e., the first item on each list is numbered “1”), but the lists can be sequentially numbered within a whole document or part of a document.

A single-step list that implies sequence can have the item numbered “1” or begin with a bullet; another option is to rewrite to avoid the single step. A consistent approach should be followed within a single document. For example, single-step lists emphasize the action item for procedures that require only a single step. An additional consideration is that numbering or bulleting a single list item helps differentiate the list item from the surrounding text.

For unordered lists, introduce each item with a bullet. Lists can be run in a paragraph or arranged vertically. Vertical arrangement is preferred for long lists or if the list items are lengthy or complex. The enumeration and demarcation of a list depends on its format and type.

List Format and Type Enumeration and Demarcation Run in paragraph and not sequential or hierarchical No enumeration: Separate items with commas or semicolons as appropriate for the complexity of the items Run in paragraph and sequential or hierarchical Enumerate with numerals within parentheses and separate items with commas or semicolons as appropriate for the complexity of the items Vertical and not sequential or hierarchical Begin each item with a bullet (•) Vertical and sequential or hierarchical Enumerate each item with a numeral followed by a period. A bullet can be used for a list with a single item.

To emphasize subsidiary items in vertically arranged lists (e.g., substeps in procedures), use sublists. No more than two levels of sublists are recommended. Ordered sublists should have numbered subitems; unordered sublists introduce subitems with a bullet.

List formats Use a vertical format if items in a list are complete sentences. Begin each item with a capital letter and end each item with a period.

The introductory sentence ends in either a colon or a period.

Correct: The following precautions are recommended during the run-in phase: • The gun should be lowered as fast as local practice dictates. • All restrictions or ID changes should be noted and speed reduced as the gun passes them.

Correct: Two precautions are recommended during the run-in phase. • The gun should be lowered as fast as local practice dictates. • All restrictions or ID changes should be noted and speed reduced as the gun passes them.

When items in a list are syntactic continuations of the introductory clause, begin each item with a lowercase letter unless the item begins with a proper noun. Place a period at the end of the last item only.

Correct: Each center provides a range of activities to support oil companies: data management, interpretation, workstations, data transfer, and communication.

Correct: Each center provides a range of activities to support oil companies: • data processing, interpretation, and archival • Interpretation Bureau Services • workstation software and equipment • worldwide transmission and communication.

Correct: Functionally test the motor as follows:

## 1. Open the rams and lower the motor

below the rotary table.

## 2. Secure the motor and the kelly.

## 3. Turn on the pumps and slowly increase

the stroke speed. If the list is embedded within a sentence, do not place a period at the end of the last item in the list.

Correct: The procedure for data exchange, reset, clear, and restart has a local meaning.

Correct: The procedure for • data exchange • reset • clear • restart has a local meaning.

Do not place a colon at the end of an introductory clause that ends in a verb or preposition.

Correct: Surface testing equipment must • safely contain well pressures • accurately measure volume, temperature, and flow rate • separate produced gas, oil, and water phases • allow collection of representative PVT- quality samples.

Visual aids Use visual aids (tables, figures, and graphs) to emphasize important ideas and to compare data.

For the latter purpose, figures or tables to be compared should be of similar scale; however, the same data should not be presented in both tables and figures. In print materials, visuals that are not merely decorative are introduced in the text. The introduction should precede the visual and help the reader interpret the visual.

For examples and guidelines on how to use visuals for print and online communications, see the “Brand Elements and Templates.” Additional resources on visuals are listed in “Suggested reading.” Figure captions and table titles Except for visuals that are purely decorative elements, each figure should have a caption and each table should have a title. For figures, both complete sentences and fragments may be used as captions in the same document, although consistency is recommended. Figure captions—even sentence fragments—have sentence-style capitalization and terminal punctuation. Table titles should be brief phrases; explanatory material can be in one or more footnotes. Table titles are usually formatted with headline-style capitalization and no terminal punctuation.

If the figures and tables are sequentially numbered, separate sequences of Arabic numerals are used and are consecutive throughout the text and within each appendix. Within each appendix, use Arabic numerals and the same letter designation as that of the appendix (A-1, A-2, etc.).

All citations of numbered illustrations and nontabular material use the abbreviation Fig. unless the word begins a sentence, in which case it is spelled out Figure. Do not use the words chart, exhibit, graph, or photograph when naming a specific figure. The word Figure is always spelled out with the number in the caption. Designate numbered tabular text visuals by Table. Designate one portion of a multipart figure with uppercase letters when the figures are separate (Fig. 2A, Fig.

2B). Use lowercase letters when referring to distinct parts of a single figure (Fig. 2a, Fig. 2b).

Figures Figure terminology The following terms are used in these figure guidelines.

Figure Element Definition Callout Descriptive term or symbol label within the figure Caption Explanatory material that is separate from but immediate to the figure

Figure Element Definition Figure title Informative description (equivalent to a text heading) within the figure that applies to all or part of figure content and does not merely duplicate the figure caption Key Part of the figure that identifies the symbols used in the figure (also called a legend) Leader line Line indicating where a callout applies Figure labeling Capitalize the first word, nouns, pronouns, adverbs, and all other words of five or more letters in a figure title.

Capitalize only the first word and proper nouns in a callout. An exception is abbreviations, which retain their capitalization. If the first element of a callout is a numeral, the first following word is not capitalized (for example, 15-ft interval).

Capitalization exceptions are company names or marks of SLB or other companies that begin with a lowercase letter.

A period is not used as terminal punctuation for figure titles and callouts except for those that are complete sentences.

Callout leader lines do not have arrowheads, which are reserved to depict motion or indicate continuing data, a directional relationship, or north.

Delete or recalculate scale ratios (for example, 1 in = 1 ft, magnification 5×) because they are not correct if the size of the graphic is different from that of the original log printout. Figure 3. QUANTUM™ PERFPAC™ single-trip perforating and gravel- and frac-pack system provides one-trip perforating and gravel packing.

Capitalize only the first word and proper nouns in an axis label on a graph. An exception is abbreviations, which retain their capitalization.

Unit values follow, separated by a comma. In rare instances an axis label consists solely of a unit value.

The axis labels are usually horizontally aligned; on three-dimensional “cube” graphs a label may be parallel to the axis if horizontal alignment distracts from the graph itself.

All symbols (including other relevant components, such as lines of calculated values) used on a graph must be identified in the text or caption or labeled on the graph, either directly with callouts or in an inset key.

Data symbols are identified in a key in hierarchical order, if one applies.

Capitalize only the first word and proper nouns in a key. An exception is abbreviations, which retain their capitalization.

Comparison of horizontal permeability measured by mini-DSTs, cores, and IPTTs shows generally good agreement of the different measurements.

Logs The track headers in the insert section of a log are edited following text guidelines. Tracks are not numbered or lettered; they are referred to by number, from left to right, although the depth track is typically not numbered but referred to as the depth track.

The log name is spelled out (do not use the mnemonic or an abbreviation) with headline- style initial capitalization and centered above an example of the log measurement curve with the scale endpoints labeled. The unit symbol is centered beneath the example curve.

Delete scale ratios (for example, 1:40 m) because they are not correct if the size of the graphic is different from that of the original log printout.

If log depths require anonymity, use letters to alias the thousands-place digits. If all depths on the depth track are the same number of digits (for example, all four-digit or all five-digit numbers), replace the thousands digits with an X for 1,000 to 9,999 (X,000 to X,999) or with XX for 10,000 and deeper (XX,000). Do not have X,000 and XX,000 in the same depth track because that reveals that the corresponding depths are obviously 9,000 and 10,000.

If a specific depth or depth interval must be differentiated in an aliased depth track, use different letters in alphabetic sequence. For example:

## XX,000

## XX,500

## XY,000

## XY,500

## XZ,000

## XZ,500

Maps This guidance also applies to satellite imagery, orthophotos, and similar metric photography. Maps used for specific technical purposes, such as positioning wells and seismic lines, typically require more detailed coordinate information.

A map should present multiple sets of information, not just location details. For example, oil and gas fields should be depicted by their approximate boundaries rather by dot symbols. Graphic elements can also be added into a map, but it’s important to avoid information overload.

In this example, two maps are used to locate the field offshore Norway. Light shading links the detailed view to the boxed area on the larger scale map. The shape of the field is consistently represented across all three elements. However, two satellite fields to the right in the FPSO graphic should also be labeled. Blue color used for fields in the detailed view implies water rather than hydrocarbons.

Location names on maps follow these conventions: All names are spelled out unless space constraints require abbreviations (see Spellings and Terminology list for abbreviations such as USA, UK, and UAE.)

Country names are written in all capital letters. State, province, basin, and field names use headline-style capitalization.

Major bodies of water are written in all capital letters; headline-style capitalization for all others. The text should curve to parallel the shoreline or river.

See guidance on names of places and guidance on capitalization.

Map orientation assumes north is toward the top of the map. If it is not, a north arrow labeled "N" should be included.

Map scales are not required for global and continental maps or other large geopolitical entities familiar to the readers. If multiple maps are in the same graphic, at least one must have a scale. Scales should be provided in both SI and oilfield customary units, with the SI units on top. Alternating colored and white bars indicate scale subunits. The unit name is centered for its scale.

Map keys must use colors, codings, and line types that exactly match the corresponding features on the map. All symbols must be explained in the key or caption. Contour units can be included on the map or in the map key.

In the key, the first word and proper nouns should be capitalized, except for abbreviations, which retain their capitalization.

Color can be used to code important map entities, such as areas in a country, fields, fault blocks, or types of production. However, states and provinces should not be color-coded; they should be defined by boundary lines.

Contours on a map must be clearly defined. At least two contours should be labeled, with every fifth interval typically shown in a heavier line weight and labeled. Contour units should be included either on the map itself or in the map key.

Tables The guidelines for table elements generally follow those for text, with concessions to the space limitations endemic to most tables. Click here for example tables.

Table terminology The following terms are used in these table guidelines.

Table Element Definition Body All vertical columns below the column headings and to the right of the stub Cut-in heading Heading that cuts across table columns and applies to all columns below it Decked heading Multilevel heading consisting of a spanner heading and at least two column subheadings with a horizontal spanner rule between the spanner and the subheadings Headline-style capitalization Capitalization of the first word, nouns, pronouns, verbs, adverbs, adjectives, and all other words of five or more letters Rule Printed line, horizontal or vertical Sentence-style capitalization Capitalization of only the first word, proper nouns, and proper adjectives

Table Element Definition Spanner heading Heading that applies to at least two columns and may have subheadings Spanner rule Horizontal line between a spanner heading and the column subheadings to which it applies Stub First (left-hand) column that lists “row headings” of categories or individual entries for which information is given in the other columns Title Caption or heading for the entire table Rules in tables Horizontal rules are typically used between table elements. If entries in the stub are indented, the corresponding rules have the same indent. A rule is used beneath a spanner or a cut-in heading to show which subheadings and columns it applies to. Vertical rules are used for complicated tables.

Column and stub headings Column headings have headline-style capitalization and no routine terminal punctuation. The only capitalization exception is abbreviations and unit symbols, which retain their capitalization.

Headings can be singular or plural. Headings are bold and left justified except for centered spanner and cut-in headings. Headings are stacked “down” from the first line.

A column heading is not required for the stub if its contents are clear from the table caption. The stub heading uses sentence-style capitalization and can be singular or plural in number. The first level of stub entries is flush left; successive sublevels are indented to the right.

If the unit symbol is the same for all entries in a column or row, it is listed at the end of the column heading or row stub, respectively, set off by a comma. If entries are presented in two sets, or dual units, the equivalent secondary unit symbol is listed in brackets following the primary unit symbol. If the table consists of more than two columns (including the stub) and the entries have dual values in primary and equivalent secondary units, the unit can be listed with each value in brackets (for example, 20 cm [8 in]) to aid the reader instead of only in the heading or stub. Tables in a document with similar subject matter or with values that are meant to be compared should use a consistent format, listing the units in the headings or stub or with the values. In rare instances a heading consists solely of a unit.

Tool Specifications Unit A Example Unit B Example Length—max., m [ft] 1.5 [5] 1.8 [6] Weight—max., kg [lbm] 24 [55] 36 [80] Tool Specifications Length—Max. Weight—Max.

Unit A example 1.5 m [5 ft] 24 kg [55 lbm] Unit B example 1.8 m [6 ft] 36 kg [80 lbm] Tool Specifications Unit A Example Unit B Example Length—max. 1.5 m [5 ft] 1.8 m [6 ft] Weight—max. 24 kg [55 lbm] 36 kg [80 lbm]

Tool Specifications Length—Max., m [ft] Weight—Max., kg [lbm] Unit A example 1.5 [5] 24 [55] Unit B example 1.8 [6] 36 [80] Table body Entries in the table body columns have sentence-style capitalization, with only the first word and proper nouns capitalized. If the first element of an entry is a numeral, the first following word is not capitalized (e.g., 15-ft interval). They are left justified, including wrapped lines, to the column heading. The exception is columns of comparable (i.e., the same measured variable), single-entry numerical values in a column, which are decimal aligned to each other with the widest entry left justified to the column heading. Columns of comparable double-entry numerical values, ranges, or entries with operational signs are left justified, not decimal aligned. Units are preferentially in the column headings or stub to save space and avoid repetition, but they may be listed instead with each value. Placement of the units in the headings or stub or with the values should be consistent within a document for similar or comparable tables.

If the table has a stub, row entries are vertically aligned to the first line of the corresponding stub entry.

Entries should be parallel for both grammar and punctuation within a column: • Words or sentence fragments on individual lines do not have terminal punctuation (including commas or semicolons). • Words or sentence fragments grouped in a paragraph are separated by commas or semicolons; there is no terminal punctuation for the paragraph. • Complete sentences on individual lines each have terminal punctuation. • Complete sentences grouped in a paragraph either each have terminal punctuation or are separated by commas or semicolons. The last sentence should have terminal punctuation.

Do not use ditto marks for repeated entries; repeat the entry or list it once, spanning the applicable columns or rows. Either a blank entry or na (for not applicable) is used if a heading does not apply or an entry would not be logical; blank entries or na must be used consistently within a document. An en dash (–) for the entry indicates that no data were available or collected. A footnote should be used to explain the meaning of blank, na, or en dash entries.

Note: When tables are created using the PIMS, some of these guidelines may not be implemented due to the system’s limitations. For example, the inability to use three-place commas, proper decimal usage, nonstacked fractions, and inline use only for superscripts and subscripts.

Numbers in tables • Use only numerals for statistical and tabular data, dates, addresses, and time. • Numbers with four or more digits are separated into groups of three digits with a comma. Commas are not used to mark off groups of three digits in dates, pages, and addresses. Commas are used in all monetary values with four or more digits.

Abbreviations in tables Check the list of “Spellings and Terminology” for the format for abbreviations and which abbreviations can be used as stand-alone terms. Abbreviations that cannot stand alone and that are

first mentioned in a table must be defined in a footnote except for common abbreviations. Most technical abbreviations and unit symbols retain their capitalization.

Footnotes in tables Footnotes should be used in the table instead of letters or numerals, which could be mistaken for exponents or chemical symbols. Footnote symbols used in tables should be used in continuous sequence when footnotes are also used in the text, and do not begin anew with each table. The symbols are assigned beginning at the upper left-hand corner of the table and reading across, row by row. A footnote symbol in a table stub entry is placed following the category, not the unit symbol at the end (for example, Yield point,† psi).

The footnote symbol is placed to the left of the margin-aligned footnote and is separated from the following text by a space. Multiple-symbol footnotes build to the left. Footnotes for a multiple- page table are placed together at the end of the table.

If the table has a Source: footnote acknowledging the source of information in a table, it is the first footnote. General table footnotes are listed next, preceded with Note: or Notes: if more than one concept is covered. Caption-, column- or entry-specific footnotes follow in the order assigned in the table.

The capitalization, punctuation, and parallelism conventions for table column entries apply to footnotes.

Table examples

References In technical publications, always identify your sources of information. References to published works (in print or electronic) are fully formatted in print or electronic documents. The “Related resources” and similar links on slb.com pages are a succinct description of the link and typically its format and file size, as described in “Writing for Our Public Websites.” References should include the following ordered information. • Author’s last name and initial(s)—List the names of all authors if there are fewer than four. If the source has four or more authors, use the first author’s name and et al.

(meaning “and others”). Follow an author’s preference for capitalization of his or her name. Initials are not spaced apart. • Title of the paper or chapter within a book or electronic document—Paper and chapter titles are in quotation marks and capitalized headline style.

• Name of the publication—Titles of journals, books, and electronic documents are italicized and capitalized headline style. If a journal title has changed, use the journal title as it was when the article was published. • Edition and name of editor (if applicable) and followed by (ed.) or (eds.). • Place of publication, name of the publisher, and date or year of publication or presentation. • Page numbers.

Do not include references for “personal communication” and unpublished works because the reader cannot readily locate these materials. Footnotes may be used to provide information for these unpublished sources.

If author and date citations are used in the text, list the references alphabetically, with all work attributed to the same author listed chronologically.

The basic elements of author and date citations are the last name of the author or authors and year of publication.

Referenced Document Author and Date Citation One author (Jones, 1995) Simulation of the field by Jones (1995) revealed that the two zones were in communication.

Two or three authors (Ehlers and Blatt, 1981) Four or more authors: List the first author’s name and et al.

(Johnson et al., 2002) Multiple documents published by the same author in the same year: Differentiate chronologically by adding letters beginning with a to the year Pettijohn and Simmons’ (1996b) second evaluation contradicted their initial findings (Pettijohn and Simmons, 1996a).

Corporate, group, or government agency: Can be cited with an acronym or abbreviation that is defined only in the reference

## (US EPA, 2000)

Personal communication (not included in the references) According to Owens (pers. comm., 1965), junk remained in the borehole after fishing.

Place double references in reverse chronological order unless circumstances dictate otherwise.

Fertl, W.: “Gamma Ray Spectral Data Assists in Complex Formation Evaluation,” The Log Analyst (September–October 1979) 20, No. 5, 3–37; also in Transactions of the 6th SPWLA European Formation Evaluation Symposium, London, England (March 26–27, 1979), paper X.

For foreign references with French, Italian, or Spanish titles, capitalize first words, proper names, and abbreviations that are normally capitalized. For German titles, capitalize only nouns. If it is unclear whether a reference is in a foreign language (for example, the title has been translated into English), put the language in parentheses after the page numbers. . . . : 34–46 (in Russian).

Electronic documents may not have all the typical elements of a traditional publication.

The reference should include the protocol and its address, access path, or directory and the date the document was accessed.

Footnotes Footnotes are used to direct readers to supplementary or explanatory material at the bottom of a page or table. Text footnotes are continuous for a document; table footnotes are independent of the text footnotes, and the footnote sequence starts anew for each table.

Use the following symbols, in order, to designate footnotes. Superscript trademark symbol numerals and letters are not preferred because they could be mistaken for exponents or chemical symbols.

Footnote symbols are separated by a comma when more than one used.

Asterisk * Double asterisk ** Dagger † Double dagger (diesis) ‡ Section mark § Two daggers †† Two double daggers ‡‡ Two section marks §§

Footnote symbols follow all punctuation except an em dash. They go within the closing parenthesis or bracket if exclusive to the parenthetical material.

Correct: The trend was not identifiable in the legacy dataset*—another survey was required to define the seismic salt boundaries.

Correct: The wells were initially treated with an acid flush,* which subsequent production logging found to have reduced permeability instead of improving it. (The footnoted material applies to the initial acid flush only.)

Correct: The wells were initially treated with an acid flush, which subsequent production logging found to have reduced permeability instead of improving it.* (The footnoted material applies to the entire statement.)

Incorrect: J. Smith†, G. Gillis‡, and K. Goy† Correct: J. Smith,* G. Gillis,** and K. Goy† In table stub entries, the footnote symbol is placed following the category, not the unit symbol at the end.

Incorrect: Yield point, psi† Correct: Yield point,* psi Clarity The hallmark of a good scientist—as well as a good writer—is attention to detail. Examine every detail from the reader’s point of view, then rewrite to sharpen, simplify, and clarify. As you rewrite, ask yourself these questions: • Are my ideas logically ordered from the simple to the complex? • Have I written simple, succinct sentences? • Have I used verbs in the active rather than passive voice? • Have I avoided shifts in subject, number, verb tense, and voice? • Have I placed subjects close to their verbs? • Have I placed verbs close to their objects? • Have I chosen words that express my meaning precisely? • Have I avoided jargon and clichés? • Have I explained all abbreviations and acronyms that cannot stand alone? • Have I eliminated needless words, especially modifiers? • Have I used correct grammar and punctuation? Have I verified numerical values, quantities, and unit symbols? Have I proofread for spelling and typographical errors?

Overview Many technical words commonly used in SLB documents are listed here. For definitions and illustrations of many of the oilfield terms, see the SLB Energy Glossary. For spelling and usage of nontechnical words, including place names, consult Merriam-Webster’s Collegiate Dictionary. SLB documents use American spelling. For additional guidance on spelling and word choice and usage, see the “Writing and Style” chapter of the Communication Handbook.

In words designated as “joined prefixes” or “joined suffixes,” the hyphen is dropped when the prefix or suffix is added to the root word (for example, “multioperation” and “fieldwide”). In words designated as “hyphenated prefixes” or “hyphenated suffixes,” the hyphen is retained when the prefix or suffix is added to the root word (for example, “self-closing” and “unit-based”). The hyphen should always be retained when joining a prefix with a word that begins with a capital letter (for example, “pro-European”) or when identical vowels or consonants come together (for example, “multi-investment” and “non-negotiable”), except with one-syllable prefixes ending in e or o (for example, “reentry”) and compound words listed as closed in Webster’s (for example, “underrun”).

For additional guidance on spelling compound words and words with prefixes and suffixes, consult Section 7.85 in the Chicago Manual of Style (16th edition). For example, most compound unit modifiers that are hyphenated when they precede a noun are not joined by a hyphen when they come after the modified noun (“decision-making guidance” and “guidance for decision making”).

Except for commonly used nontechnical abbreviations (for example, “US” and “a.m.”) and abbreviations identified as “stand alone” by the dagger footnote, always spell out the term or phrase at first use with the abbreviation enclosed in parentheses following the term. Thereafter, the abbreviation is used alone. Stand-alone abbreviations do not require introduction with the spelled- out term or phrase unless readers will not readily recognize them. Minor variations to what an abbreviation stands for can be read in context, not reintroduced (for example, “POOH” is spelled out on first mention as “pull out of hole” but used later in the document for “pulled out of hole”). However, plurals must be formed from the abbreviation itself (for example, the plural of “BHA” for “bottomhole assembly” is “BHAs”).

The abbreviations on this list are repeated in a sublist for ease of reference.

Overview Along with commonly used nontechnical abbreviations (for example, “US” and “a.m.”), the following “stand-alone” abbreviations do not require spelling out the technical term or phrase at first use with the abbreviation enclosed in parentheses following the term. However, if readers will not readily recognize an abbreviation, introduce it with the spelled-out term or phrase as a courtesy. The abbreviations identified with a dagger footnote can stand alone in tables or sidebar bullet lists but should be fully introduced on the first text mention.

The list is in two parts. The first is organized by the spelled-out terms; the second, by the abbreviations.

Unit Name Unit Symbol Comment acceleration due to gravity (g-force) gn Use this SPE- and IEEE-accepted unit symbol; do not use G for acceleration due to gravity 1 gn = 9.806650E+00 m/s2 acre acre (see “m2 or m2” or “ha”) Use of m2 or m2 for square meter or ha for hectare preferred; 1 acre = 4.046856E+03 m2 = 4.046856E– 01 ha 1 acre (US survey) = 4.046873E+03 m2 = 4.046873E–01 ha acre-foot acre.ft (see “m3 or m3” or “ha.m”) Use of m3 or m3 for cubic meter or ha.m for hectare- meter preferred; 1 acre.ft = 1.233489E+03 m3 = 1.233489E–01 ha.m ampere A ampere per meter A/m Do not use Oe for oersted; 1 Oe = 7.957747E+01 A/m ampere-hour A.h (see “kC”) Use of kC for kilocoulomb preferred; 1 A.h = 3.6E+00 kC ångstrom (10–10 m) See “nm” Use nm for nanometer; 1 Å = 1.0E–01 nm Do not use Å for ångstrom annum (sidereal year) a Use only in combined units for measured time values (e.g., m/a); use allowable customary unit yr for year only in combined units (e.g., m3/yr) Spell out “year” for values used in a descriptive or literary manner API (American Petroleum Institute) gamma ray gAPI API gravity API Do not use °API, dAPI, or degAPI for API gravity API neutron nAPI atmosphere (standard) atm (see “MPa” or “kPa”) Use of MPa for megapascal or kPa for kilopascal preferred; 1 atm = 1.01325E–01 MPa = 1.01325E+02 kPa Standard conditions are 760-mm Hg at 0 degC or 14.696 lbf/in2 atomic mass unit See “u” Use u for unified atomic mass unit, which is equal to 1/12 of the mass of an atom of the nuclide 12C 1 u = 1.6605402 × 10–27 kg with a combined standard uncertainty of 0.0000010 × 10–27 kg† Do not use amu for atomic mass unit bar bar (see “MPa” or “kPa”) Use of MPa for megapascal or kPa for kilopascal preferred; 1 bar = 1.0E–01 MPa = 1.0E+02 kPa barn b (see “fm2 or fm2”) The unit of area used to measure the reaction cross section of atomic nuclei and subatomic particles.

Not to be confused with “B” for bel, the unit of sound intensity, or for byte, the unit of information in computer storage and processing.

Use of fm2 or fm2 for square femtometer preferred; 1 b = 1.0E+02 fm2†

Unit Name Unit Symbol Comment gigawatt (thermal) GWth (see “MWth”) A unit of power measurement, representing one billion watts of heat energy delivered. Use preferred to measure the thermal energy produced by a power plant. Often used in the context of nuclear reactors where thermal energy is converted to electrical energy.

Example: The reactor produces 3.0 GWth of thermal energy. gigawatt-hour GWh (see “MWh”) A unit of energy measurement, representing one billion watt-hours. It is commonly used to express the output power of engines, the power consumption of large-scale electrical devices, and the capacity of power plants and other electrical infrastructure.

Unit Name Unit Symbol Comment megawatt (thermal) MWth (see “GWth”) A unit of power that quantifies the rate at which heat energy is delivered.

It is used to measure the heat output of geothermal and other thermal power plants. megawatt-hours MWh (see “GWh”) A unit of energy representing the total amount of electrical energy delivered over one hour. It is used to quantify energy delivery over specified periods such as daily, monthly, or yearly.

For geothermal power, plant output is measured in megawatts (MW) or gigawatts (GW), depending on the scale. MW and GW are preferred for larger geothermal projects, while kW is typically used for smaller-scale applications. meter m Do not spell the unit name “metre” (SLB style uses American spelling: Merriam Webster’s Collegiate Dictionary, 11th ed.)

To use sack as a unit, specify the material and its volume in m3 or m3 for cubic meter (preferable to characterizing it by its mass).

Use of L/metric ton for liter per metric ton is preferred to galUS/sack for gallon per sack to express the volume of fluid per mass of solid (specific volume).

Use of L/m3 or L/m3 for liter per cubic meter is preferred to galUS/sack for gallon per sack to express the volume of fluid per volume of solid (concentration).

Consider converting bel to decibel (dB): 1 dB = 0.1 B; 1 B = 10 dB) to avoid confusion. b (see “fm2 or fm2”) barn The unit of area used to measure the reaction cross section of atomic nuclei and subatomic particles.

To use sack as a unit, specify the material and its volume in m3 or m3 for cubic meter (preferable to characterizing it by its mass). Use of L/metric ton for liter per metric ton preferred to galUS/sack for gallon per sack to express the volume of fluid per mass of solid (specific volume). Use of L/m3 or L/m3 for liter per cubic meter preferred to galUS/sack for gallon per sack to express the volume of fluid per volume of solid (concentration).

Do not use sk for sack

Unit Symbol Unit Name Comment scf (use restricted: see “ft3 or ft3”) standard cubic foot Use ft3 or ft3 and specify the standard conditions at which the value was measured Except for gas, use of m3 or m3 for cubic meter or dm3 or dm3 for cubic decimeter preferred; 1 ft3 = 2.831685E–02 m3 = 2.831685E+01 dm3 For gas, use Bscf for billion standard cubic feet, Mscf for thousand standard cubic feet, MMscf for million standard cubic feet, and Tscf for trillion standard cubic feet only if the standard conditions at which the value was measured are specified; express the value without scientific notation If standard conditions are not specified for gas, use Bcf for billion cubic feet, Mcf for thousand cubic feet, MMcf for million cubic feet, and Tcf for trillion cubic feet scf/d (use restricted: see “ft3/d or ft3/d”) standard cubic feet per day Use ft3/d or ft3/d and specify the standard conditions at which the value was measured Except for gas, use of m3/h or m3/h for cubic meter per hour or m3/d or m3/d for cubic meter per day preferred; the value is expressed without scientific notation If standard conditions are not specified for gas, use Bcf/d for billion cubic feet per day, Mcf/d for thousand cubic feet per day, MMcf/d for million cubic feet per day, and Tcf/d for trillion cubic feet per day sec (not permitted: see “s”) second Use s for second seca second (angle) Do not use ″ (double prime) for second sg specific gravity Specific gravity uses water as the reference density (1 g/cm3 = 1.0E+03 kg/m3); Unit Prefixes SI Unit Prefixes The SI unit prefixes are used to form decimal multiples and submultiples of SI units. They are joined to the unit names (e.g., kilometer, not kilo-meter). Their use with customary unit abbreviations and unit names should be avoided.

SI Unit Prefixes Prefix Symbol Factor yotta Y 1024 zetta Z 1021 exa E 1018 peta P 1015 tera T 1012 giga G 109 mega M 106 kilo k 103 hecto h 102 deka da 101 deci d 10–1 centi c 10–2 milli m 10–3 micro 10–6 nano n 10–9 pico p 10–12 femto f 10–15 atto a 10–18 zepto z 10–21 yocto y 10–24

Customary Unit Prefixes These prefixes are used strictly with specified customary unit symbols.
